PROOF • POSITIVE. —A gentleman employed a carpente: to partition off a part of his study and particularly instructed the workman to make the partition sound proof. The carpenter declared that he could do this effectually with a filling of sawdust. When it was finished, the gentleman stood on one side and called to the carpenter on the other. "Can you hear me, Smith ? " "No, sir; not a bit," was the prompt reply. SUNDAY SCHOOL TEACHER "Who loves every- body, Johnnie ?" Johnnie :1" My pa does, cos he's trying to get into the Town Council." A PERSONAL QUESTION.—AB old man who believed that "what was to be would be," lived in the West, and was one day going out several miles through a region infested by very savage Indians. He always took his gun with him, but this time found that one of the tamily had it out. As he would not go out without it, some of his friends tantlised him by saying there was no danger of the Indians: that he would not die until his time came, anyhow." "Yes," said the- old fellow: "but suppose? was to meet an Indian, and his time had come; I wouldn't do, nohow, not to have my gun." MISS LAURA: "Oh, auntie! You remember Mr. Meeker, who went from here as a missionary? I have just heard that those awful cannibals ate him." Aunt Sophronia "La, me! Ido hope they cooked him with turnips. The poor dear man was so fond of turnips, you know." DURING a lecture on architecture, the tempera- ture of the room was very hot. Just as the heat was almost unbearable, the lecturer continued "And now we will turn to Greece." "You're right," said one of the audience, who was freely perspiring, "we will turn to grease if we don't have some of these windows opened." A WORTHY man who was very sensitive and retiring, having lost his wife, privately requested that he might be remembered in the minister's morning prayer from the pulpit, but asked that his name might not be mentioned. On Sunday morning the good minister prayed most eloquently for "our aged brother upon whom the heavy hand of sore affliction has so lately fallen." At this point an elderly man, whom the minister had married to a very young wife during the week, rose with a bounce, and stamped down the aisle, muttering loud enough to be heard half over the chapel —"it: may be an affliction, but I'm bleat if I want to be prayed for in that fashion! "CAPITAL" CHAT.

Americans believe in London as an alternative to Paris more and more every year. In acknowledg- ment of their presence, one Regent-street shop boldly exhibits "The Fashions of the Fall," and in another window, everything is marked in dollars instead of in pounds, and Mr. Peter Robinson calls his establishment a "dry goods store."

There are no fewer than fifty-four men in the present House of Commons who have written books. That is as near as possible one in twelve. Roughly speaking, social and political takes twenty; travel, six; history, eight; philosophy and essays, nine; education, tour; law, four; the army and navy, three; colonies, three; trade, three; fiction, six; literature, one; medicine, one; and poetry, two.

The rumour concerning the engagement of Prin- cess Maud of Wales to Prince Christian of Denmark is exciting much interest in his country. The Copenhagen papers state that the engagement will be publicly announced about the middle of Octo- ber, for up to now the announcement has not been authenticated. The Danes believe that the Queen of Denmatk's prejudice against the marriage of first-ccusins would be a barrier to the match. It is thought that the Princess and her cousin have long entertained a deep affection for each other.

In the short session of Parliament which has just closed Mr. Chamberlain has used his few opportu- nities well, and spoken with strength and definite- ness when he has had to speak at all. But it has been noticeable that from the first his appearances in the House and his rising in debate have never , been welcomed by the cheers freely given to Mr. Balfour and others. Mr. Balfour has treated him with the most charming courtesy and consideration at all times, and has, so far as can be judged, abso- lutely ignored the sometimes visible annoyance on the Treasury bench at Mr. Chamberlain's perform- ances. Mr. Chamberlain, on the other hand,has on all occasions ostentatiously asserted what he con- ceives to be his right to be a joint leader with Mr. Balfour. And he is so pertinacious that he is not unlikely to succeed. If Mr. Balfour is not in the , House when Mr. Chamberlain arrives, the latter pushes on till he is half sitting on the exec', spot ' of the bench usually taken by the First Lord on the side furthest from the Speaker. This seat be- yond the leader of the House has invariably been cccupied by the Chancellor of the Exchequer. Sir Michael Hicks-Beach has in this short session been obliged usually to content himself with a seat next Mr.Goschen and on the left of Mr. Balfour. On two or three occasions when he came in early enough, or just after a divi-ion, Sir Michael has asserted his right,but has shortly after found himself supplanted by Mr. Chamberlain.

These transparent little tactics have been freely commented upon by both sides of the louse in the lobbies and tearooms. Some members are confident that Mr. Balfour, with his tact and natural ascend- ancy, will end by putting Mr. Chamberlain in his place, and will convince him that the Unionist Party is to be guided by Tory and not by Birming- ham policy. Others back Mr. Chamberlain's un- sparing determination to have his own way, anil hold that the open and avowed nature of Mr. Chamberlain's scheme for asserting the dual con- trol, so far from being a sign of weakness, is only an outward manifestation of his method. They contend that Mr. Chamberlain has always studied the policy, not of winning by being liked, but of winning by being feared, and that Tories, being mainly phlegmatic and indisposed to pertinacious resistance, will end by knocking under to this strategy. Time will show.

He offered his services to the Lendon Missionary Society, which accepted them, and tv. has since laboured in Africa. Mr. Willoughby has excellent qualities for ,be management, of so delicate and important a mission as has now brought him to this country.

Colonel Stitt, the governor of General Booth's farm colony at Hadleigh, has just returned from a visit to Canada. He was one of the members of the .mall body of Salvationists, headed by Mr. Herbert Booth, who have been carrying out investigations ,n the north-west provinces in refprence to a pro- bable site for the Salvationist over-sea colony. The reoommendstions resulting from the inquiry are understood to he favourable, and a deciAnn in the matter may be looked for shortly.

If what I hear is correct there need Ile no surprise nt this. When Mr. Chamberlain .xporinded hi .= policy at Birmingham and dwelt upon the particular point,a person in the body of the hall wanted to know whether the figures as to the repaytnent of purchase money which Mr. Chamber- lain was expounding left the working men ultim- ately as the freeholders or only the leaseholders of their dwellings. The question was certainly not cleared no quite satisfactorily at the time. and I ,indprstand that, whatever may be the noosibilities of devising a practical scheme, the figures Mr. Chamberlain gave were a good deal too rosy.

The" British Medical Journal "contains an inter- estinct account of Miss Hamilton, the lady doctor who acts as personal physician to the Amedr, and is in attendance on the Shalizada, with strict injunctions to accompany him everywhere. Miss Hamilton started on her medical career as a trained nurse in the infirmary of the Liverpool workhouse. Afterwards proceeding to the London Medical School for Women. In 1890 she qualified in Glas- gow, Edinburgh. and Brussels, going thence to Cal- cutta, where she established herself in private practice with great success.

It was owing to illness that she first went up to the hills, and so on to Cahul. In the August of 1894 the Ameer first sent for Miss Hamilton, who nursed him through an attack of gout so serious that she hardly ever left his room, and did not have more than two hours' sleep at a time. She has, she says, at all times met with great consideration at the hands of the &raper. the only discomforts she has suffered arising from the marked differences between F,Rstern and Western modes of living. Nor is Miss Hamilton without honour in her own country, for she has had an audience of the Queen and a kind letter from the Secretary for India ex- pressing the desire that everything should be done , for her comfort.

The :women of America are much more enterprising and pushing than their English sisters. We do not possess a female engineer on this side of the Atlantic, so far as we know, but the ladies of America are crowding into even this masculine Profession, it appears. An American lady who trades" under the name a Mrs. Henry D. Cram, and who dwells in the classic city of Boston, is an engineer, and, furthermore, seems to be gifted with all the go-ahead qualities of her race. She has just concluded a contract to supply all the steam and hand derricks required in the erection of the buildings for the Paris! Exposition in 1900.

Who rules in Russia ? According to the Russian Press. Czar Nicholas IL But Ellgfill'S neighbours have their doubts. The correspondents of German and Austrian papers declare that the Czar no longer receives ministers of State. and sees none but mem- bers of his own family. He remains in the seclu- sion of Peterhoff Palace and passes his time read- ing, mostly English and German books. Both the Czar and his young spouse are suff-ring from nervousness, brought on, it is said, by the continu- ous appearance of threatening letters and pam- phlets smuggled into their rooms. How much is t^ue in all this nobody knows, but it is certain that the Czar lives very secludedly.

graphy by V‘in der Weyde, in 1876. In 1876 the J.blochoff electric candle was invented and shown to the French Academy. One history of costumes give 1847 different styles of head-dresses for women. Rush-matting should be washed with clean soft watt r and salt, in order to prevent it from turning yellow. To keep apples through the winter in a barrel, bore holes in the bottom arid sides of the barrel and store ot.l a dry platform a foot or more high. The Queen of Madagascar, although she is thirty-four years ot age, has an eighteen-inch waist. A French paper says so. A little ammonia in tepid water will soften and cleans the skin. Cold biscuits may be successfully warmed by dipping each one in hot water and setting it on the grate in abet oven for a few minutes. Dust the grate first.

The height of waves in the sea has often been eitaggi r- ated, owing to the difficulty of measuring them : but the highest authentic observation is about forty feet. An unusually solemn double wedding took place in a handsome Brooklyn home last week. The wedding wa, a result of the desire of Mr. Gaybert Barnes to see ids daughters, Bessie and Rachael,married before his deal h, which was belle.-ed to be imminent. The brides were given away and blessed by their dying father. A doctor is said to have discovered the curious fact that the skull of a man who has died from delirium tremens contains alcoholic vapour. A small pening in the skull ' soon after death ' permits the alcohol, he tells us, to escape, when it can be ignited, and burns with a bluish flame.

The " Realm "tells a story about the late Lady Rose- bery. Having tripped up over a carpet in the drawing- room one day, she rang the bell and told the butler to nail it doe n. He replied that he would tell the footma,i at once. The footman came, looked at the carpet, and said he would send the upper-housemaid. After a long interval that domestic arrived, and said she would send under-housemaid, who, in her turn, said she would send the Buttons. In the end, Lady Rosebery nailed down the carpet herself. The" fluman,tarian " gives some particulars ab)ut the work done by the fem de convicts in Woking Prl.,on, which is the only female convict establishment in Eng- land. The inmates at one time usf dtodo a good deal of mosaic work for flooring,and the floor of the cry pt at St. Paul's, the Duke of Corms uebt's summer house. and some of the floors at Sandringham are the product of prison labour. There is a laundry employing about eighteen hands, but the largest industry at Woking Is twine-making, which employs twenty women. The twine is used at the Post Office.

The Voice. Many vocal authorities maintain that the voice is greatly affected by diet, and Dr. Pegg, who was once an associate of Liszt, expresses a strong confirmation of this view. Where fish is the chiet article of food, fine vocalists are scarce, and the voice in ordinary speech lacks delicacy of timbre and dignity. Dr. Pegg main- tains that the most desirable food for singers is of a grarninivorcus kind, with occasional indulgence in a moderate quantity of meat. He discountenances the idea that malt liquors give strength to the voice, al- though they may stimulate it for a short time. Singer.; who wou'd keep their tones fresh, rich, and long, should be chary of alcohol in any form. Smoking is not look ,d upon as injurious to the voice, though if the habit is not kept well in subjection it may affect the strength of the vocal organs.

Dental Degeneration. All over the civilised world the degeneration of the teeth of the children of to-day has become alarming. Canada is about to introduce regular dental inspection of the teeth of the children in her public schools, with a view of remedying the results of neglect on the part of the parents. A mQvement is on foot to make similar inspection compulsory in the public schools of this country. The teeth of the poor are almost universally bad, and in most cases, practically the only attention the teeth ever get is when they are pulled out. Bad and insufficient diet lowers the vital forces, dental caries set in, and with it a tendency to eschew proper foods and seek for softer and more highly-seasoned nutriment This makes the matter worse, for working people should sat food that requires considerable mastication—so- called strong food, like black bread and beans, upon which German peasants thrive so well. Dr. Newton has I found that nlor people will not go to a dentist, even I when urged to do BO by their medicel adviser, because of the expense. tie believes that in a few years such facilities will be pr wided that the poor will be able to have their teeth lo )ked to gratuitously.

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAREST NELLIE,

Of course y3u will be taking part, or at any rate going to some bazaars this season. The season seems to have set in already with some severity, and sundry charities have caught fashion on the wing, and managed to enlist and interest some very great people. in deserving charities. A dress that showed up well and looked cool and pretty at the opening of a London hospital had both skirt and bodice of tine cream serge, and the bodice was but- toned into place over a tight•fitting lining with neat silver buttons placed round the armholes. The sleeves, collar, and central box pleat were all of shot green silk with large silver buttons down the pl at; and the hat was of green crinkled straw with a green silk crown. The brim was ewered with white accordeon pleated lisse, and there were white ostrich feathers and white ribbon loops.

As regards blouses, surah is a material which makes very stylish-looking garments. Crepon, however, still holds its own, and here is a neat blouse made of lemon colour and white striped crepon, plain or figured cambric. Zephyr, sateen or drill would be equally suitable. It is fastened with small buttons in the centre of front through a single box-pleat, the fullness each side being gathered at the neck and waist. The back is stitched between the lower edge of a small pointed yoke of double material, the slight fulness below being drawn to the figure by means of a tape run through a casing, stitched across the back of waist, which is completed with a full band of material. A high collar turned back with a point each side finishes the neck. The full gigot sleeves are gathered into the armhole.

Returning to what I said just now about urah as a material for blouses, I may tell you of a sweetly-pretty one I saw in this material at a society function this week. The colour was black, with collar and cuffs of finely tucked white lawn, Valenciennes lace insertion and frills. The hat was of black net, the edge and crown of fancy straw. It is trimmed with roses and pleated Idack net, and has an aigrette of cream lace.

As for millinery, extreme width still remains the listinctive feature of the smartest hats, the width in some instances being so exaggerated as to cause the wearer to look ridiculous. These remarks, how- ever, do not apply, I am pleased to say, with reference to bonnets ; many are as a matter of fact somewhat pronounced in width, but generally speaking exaggerated forms of trimming are in the minority. Bonnets emanating from the best houses are built with due regard to the indi- vidualism of the wearer, extravagant styles are modified, and the colour or colours chosen are those that will show the complexion to the greatest ad- vantage. An elegant bonnet of medium proportions worn last week by one of fashion's leaders in society is composed of corn-coloured straw with trimmings of butter-coloured lappet lace, cornflowers, and white double narcissus ; the strings in this instance were of green, matching exactly the twist of tulle and the blade grass. One could not do other than admire this bonnet, for it was void of extreme style, and yet looked so distinguished, that I really think in its simplicity partly lies its attractiveness. Then there is a very fashionable hat of fine Leg- horn straw. Its crown is hidden by white satin bows, from which spring two upright plumes in the centre and two drooping over the brim on either side.

Jackets? Yes, I think I did promise to tell you something about then, this week. I shall content myself, though, with describing to you one of the most stylish models. It was made of dust colour covert coating, trimmed with a darker shade of braid. It is tight-fitting to just below the waist, where the box-pleated basque is secured. Each side of the front is trimmed with a row of braid to meet in the centre, where it fastens invisibly with hooks to the waist, which is trimmed round with a row of braid. The circular collarette is edged entirely round with braid, and secured to the neck under a high collarband edged with the same. The aleeves are out with a seam at the back from wrist to the elbow, where the fullness is gathered and secured, the wrist being trimmed with braid to form a pointed cuff, the upper part is trimmed to correspond, and the fulness at top pleated into the armhole.

Your loving JENN IL

THE SHAHZADA'S IMPRESSIONS OF

ENGLAND.

e nS si ra tAncrgudaiyn evening ft vhnemA gohnadnn na g. e nltn, tahr er i cv oe ud risne Po ie. rai ns interview with a representative of Reuter's Agency, Sir Acquin described some of the impressions of England which Nasrulla Khan has received. Both the Amcor and the Shataada, he said, have expressed their great grati- fication at the visit to Great Britain, and both have been touched by the cordiality and hospitality displayed by the people everywhere. The Shahzada has been pro- foundly impressed with the greatness of England,especi- ally as shown by her manufactures and commerce, and the abundant signs of her peaceful prosperity. His Highness has often remarked that Englishmen seem to be absorbed in their personal affairs and to take com- paratively little interest in military matters. The aver- age man, he has noticed with surprise, knows nothing about handling a sword, and, apparently, does not even possess one. With regard to the military displays he has seen, the Shahzada felt that, although they were impos- ing REI a spectacle, they afforded in themselves clear evi- dence of peaceful tendencies. He remarked in this con- nection, "If the strength of England were maintained by fighting prowess alone the aspect of the troops would have been less decorative, but,no doubt, equally imposing to a prince of a military nation like mv own." The Shah- zada has on many occasions said in Sir Acquin Martin how greatly touched he was with his reception by the Great Qqeen," for whom he had the deepest venera- tion. ' The kindness shown to him by all the members of the Royal Family and the responsible officers of the Crown has profoundly impressed him. Questioned as to the probable political results of the visit, and con- cerning the Government's reply to the Ameer's request to be officially represented in London, Sir Acquin re- plied :—" There can be no doubt whatever that the Shahzada's visit will be productive of infi- nite goad and be far-reaching in its effects. Regarding the Ameer's request, I cannot say more than that I do not doubt that when the moment is opportune the British Government will find a way to meet Abdur Rahman Khan's wishes." In conclusion, Sir Acquin said i---" I wish to say that nine-tenths of the stories which have been published about the Shahzada, his do- ings, his relations with the Government, and also about his stay at Dorchester House, are not only untrue but absurd. His Hi4huess did not come to England for pleasure alone. He was entrusted by his father with an impertant mission,and when that was completed he lett. During big stay on the ContioeLt—in fact, from the ms- ment he left England until he again gees on board the Clive—he has ceased to be the guest of the Govern- ment."

GLENALP INES OATH;

OR, THE MARK OF THE BLACK CROSS. - - Br SYLVANUB COBB, JB.

CHAPTER XII.

A REVELATION-A SURPRISE IN THE JUNGLE.

"Peter, what man is that standing over against yonder wall?" Lord Birchmear asked the question of Peter Brim. They had just come out upon the veranda, and were ready to set forth towards Jungeepoor— for his lordship had determined to take Peter with him. He had come to trust the old keeper above all the other servants who had come out from Eng- land with him; he was one of the very few links of domestic arrangement that connected the present with the past. Peter looked in the direction pointed out, and when his eye had rested upon the individual who had attracted his master's attention he started like one awe-stricken. He beheld the same heavily bearded face, the same tall, gaunt form, clad in its semi-barbarous hunting-garb, and the same deep. sunken, preternaturally gleaming eyes that had appeared more than once at the Lodge at Birch. mear.

.......... " Why ?" ejaculated the old servitor, "he must have flown through the air. He did not come with us; and yet I saw him in England the day before we left."

"There is no marvel in that, Peter. Another ship has come in since our arrival. But who is he ? ' "That, my lord, is the man called Glenalpine, who some men say, is in league with spirits of another world.'

"Art sure, Peter ?" demanded Birchmear, eagerly. "Is it he who was once known as Sir Philip ?" "The same, my lord." "Peter, go tell that man that I would speak with him. Address him kindly, and assure him that I mean him well."

Peter started down from the veranda, but ere he had reached the street the strange man had disappeared. He went over by the wall and looked in every direction, but the waif was nowhere to be seen.

" Strange ! strange I" murmured Sir John, when his servant had reported the result. "That man has haunted Birchmear. My lady has seen him several times."

"Did you ever know him, my lord?" "I think .I knew him in London many years ago. He was then very young, and had just come into possession of his fine estate in Ireland. He was a rollicking, free-hearted fellow, inclined to spend money faster than his banker could furnish it. Afterwards I heard that a great calamity had be- fallen him; but I have no knowledge of his life since those early days. If you should see him, Peter, and can speak with him, tell him that Sir John Berwick would like to see him; and be sure that you make him unuerstand that I will be his friend."

Peter promised; and after this the two mounted their horses and rode away. Other servants, with pack-horses, had gone on in advance. Below one of the water-batteries of Fort William they took a gondola and crossed the river, and at a garden. house, or café, near the grounds of the Bishop's Collegelthey stopped. This was a fashionable resort —a sort of free OMb-house—where gentlemen found various and well-appointed arrangements tor en- tertainment and amusement. Sir John left Peter In care of the horses while he entered the building. He was gone some time, and when he came forth his face wore a troubled and bewildered ex- pression. He held a bank-note in his hand, which he had apparently crumpled up awl smoothed out again; and he gazed upon it as though there was something about it that troubled him. At length he drew forth his pocket-book, and laid the note into one of the folds, after which he resumed his seat in the saddle.

"Which way now, my lord ?" asked Peter, as Sir John hesitated.

The baron started out from a deep reverie, and presently replied: "Straight on, Peter. We shall find our train at Berampoor." They rode for some distance at a brisk trot, but when the settlement had been left behind, and they had entered the country, Sir John drew in his rein. The road at this point skirted a deep jungle, and in advance was a long, gentle acclivity, up which the horses were allowed to walk at a slow pace. Not a word had yet been spoken since leaving the town. Peter would have asked many questions concerning the various objects they had passed, but he had noticed that his master was absorbed in his own reflections, and he cared not to disturb him. At length, however, the baron spoke, and as he did so he made a movement as though he would shake off a burden that oppressed him. "Peter, I think you are a faithful old servant." The keeper looked up with surprise. At first there was a shade of reproof upon his honest face; but gradually he seemed to comprehend that the remark had been an assertion, rather than a query, and he was relieved.

_ " I think you have known me long enough to prove me, my lord." "You can keep a secret, Peter ?" 'I can keep it with my life, if there is need." "I am in trouble, Peter, and I may want assist- ance,—and I most have the assistance of some one who is att“ched to me. I think you are that man." "You KNOW it, Sir John I" All the oaths that ever were sworn could not have carried such weight of proof as did that simple expression. Lord Birchmear returned his servitor a grateful look as he responded : "If I had not thought so, Peter, I should not have selected you, of all the others, for your present office. I shall trust you ; and you will remember that 'family secrets are sacred. You will rqi- a seal upon your lips." " You need not fear, my lord." "Peter," pursued his lordship, after a brief pause, "I told you I was in trouble. Did you know that Arthur, before he left Birchmear the last time, robbed me—that he took from my cabinet a large sum of money ?" "My lord !" exclaimed the old servitor, in blank surprise. "Arthur—robbed you ?" 114 You never heard of it?"

"No, air. You never spoke of it in my hearing before."

"No ; I have spoken of it to no one. I did not wish it known."

"And you believe, my lord, that Arthur did that thing ?" There was a flash of indignation in the old man's grey eyes, and his look was earnest. "I could not help believing it, Peter. The thing was plain. And yet, God only knows what a ter- rible burden upon my belief it was." Peter Brun trembled from head to foot. "My lord, will you tell me what proof you had ?" "The money was missed immediately after Arthur went away, and at several places where he stopped on the road I found bank-notes which he had paid out, and which I recognised as having been taken from my cabinet. I had the number of every note." "And you have never fallen tipon any circum- stance which could point to somebody else as having taken that money ?" questioned Peter, with an anxiety which he could not conceal. Sir John was for a time silent. At length he said with a reluctance which would seem to imply that it cost him an effort thus to confide such matters to a servant, "Something has transpired here in Calcutta, Peter, which puzzles me' though it does not necessarily imply a new direction of evidence. Yesterday I received from Colonel Tabor a fifty- pound note, and upon examining it I discovered it to be one of those which I had lost. You know how the notes of the Bank of England are num- bered ?"

Peter said he knew, and the baron pro- ceeded : "The note was quite new, and not to be mis- taken. I asked the colonel where he had taken it, and he informed me, at the Bishop's College Club- House. I stopped there, as you know, and showed the note to the keeper. From the peculiar character of the place, and the various ranks and positions of those who visit him, he has found it convenient to keep a private memorandum of the numbers of the larger bank-notes, together with the names of the parties of whom he receives them, and of those to whom he pays them. This note be had received farm Harold Berwick !" Peter nodded as though he had expected as much. "You are not surprised, Peter? I suppose you think that the note may have been originally paid out in Calcutta by Arthur ?" "1 don't think it necessary to suppose any such thing, my lord. But I'd like to ask you if this is the first money you have found in India of that kind? "It is not, Peter. 1 have found two °the and of course I suppose that Arthur mui passed them; but last night I learned from (

Harlow, of the Golden Face, that Arthur exchalge* all his notes for gold in Bristol." "Well, my lord," said the old servitor, with a strange manifestation of eager interest, "and what do you gather from all this?" "That is what troubles me, Peter. I don't know what to make of it. 0! if I could think that the poor boy had been traduced—that he was not a robber, after all, I should be— But the evi- dence is—" "All on one side, so far as your lordship has heard it," broke in Peter, resolutely. "My lord, I never knew till now that Mr. Arthur had been accused of stealing that money. I never heard a lisp of it." Because," explained the baron, "I did not wish any one to know it. Her ladyship and Har- old were the only two to whom I communicated the secret. They helped me to trace the money to Arthur's possession." "Aye," responded Peter, with great vehemence, "and none could have done it better." " Peter I" "My lord, it isn't for servants to take upon themselves the affairs of the master's house, and especially it isn't for them to unearth things that could only make trouble and bad feelings. If I had known that Arthur was suspected of having taken that money, I could have told you different long ago." Lord Birchmear turned suddenly in his saddle, ' and caught his henchman by the arm, and at the movement both horses stopped. "Peter I What do you mean? Don't venture to trifle with me. Let not your love for the boy 1 lead you into assertions that cannot be verified." "My lord, I shall only tell you what I know." "And if it can lift this load from the shoulders of my boy, why have you not told it before ?" "First, my lord, I did not know that the burden I was upon the boy's shoulders, and in the next place it was a matter of which I had no right to speak, as you will see. I will tell it now, and I trust you will bear me blameless, and shield me from the enmity of others." "Go on," said Sir John eagerly. "Give me back my hope of Arthur and I will bless you." "I shall tell you what I know, my lord, and you can draw your own conclusions. You remember the night at Birchmear when Major Thorpe and his friends were there—the night when the young men caroused and quarrelled in the old donjon ?" "I remember," said the baron, with a shudder. "It was Arthur's last night at the manor." "On that night," continued Peter, "as the ' party in the donjon were roysterous I determined to sit up until all was quiet, since you had particu- larly enjoined it upon me to be watchful of fire. It was long past midnight when the revel was given over, and when I had seen the lights out in the donjon I bethought me that you and the major had sat quite late over your bottle in the library. Of course I knew that your lordship always kept a clear head, but still I fancied it no harm just to take a look that way to make sure that no stray sp.lik had fallen among your papers. I had entered the library and assured myself that all was right, and was thinking whether I would gather up the few empty bottles that stood upon the table and carry them away, when I heard footsteps in the outer passage. Supposing it was you, my lord, and not caring that you should find me surveying the array of empty bottles, I shut the hood of my lantern and slipped behind the fire. Screen. "Instead of your lordship, however, it was your lady who entered with a lighted candle in her hand. Of course I couldn't retreat then, and I was forced to remain where I was. I saw her go to the old cabinet and set the candle down on the writing- ledge. She unlocked a small drawer, and I saw that she was taking money from it—new, crisp bank-notes—and she counted them out until she counted two thousand pounds. Just then my foot slipped on the floor, and her ladyship started and dropped the notes. She was terribly frightened, and her face was pale as death. But she heard nothing more, and presently she picked up the fallen money, and having shut the drawer and closed the cabinet, she sent away. "But that isn't all, my lord. Not long after- wards I saw Harold come out from her ladyship's boudoir. I won't deny that I was acting the spy then. Things didn't look right tome, and I wanted to see what was going on. Harold came out from the boudoir, and I followed him to the chamber of Mr. Arthur. He went in, and was gone a few minutes, and then came out and went away to his own room." "Did you hear him converse with Arthur? " asked the baron. " No, sir. Poor Arthur was fast asleep, as I could tell by his heavy snoring. Mr. Harold had other business in that chamber." "And you never told of this before, Peter? "How could I, my lord? I knew not then what horrible plot had been hatched up against our boy; and of course it wasn't in my place to betray what I'd discovered in such a manner, and of my master's own wife and—" Sir John put up his hand. "No more, Peter You did quite right. And yet—my God! what a piece of work this has been I" Lord Bit chmear had listened to Ida attendant's story very much as a patient might submit to a severe surgical operation, which grew more and more painful as the knife cut deeper and deeper. He had comprehended the whole when the story was concluded, and had no questions to ask. "My lord, you do not blame me?" "I have only to thank you, my friend." "And no harm shall come to me from Harold or

her—" "Hush 1 I trust they may never know how this information reached me." _ . ..

And alter this master and man rode on in silence for a long time. Occasionally marks of pain were apparent upon Sir John's face, but for the most part his expression was of great relief. &foie night they reached Serarnpoor, where two servants had arrived with the luggage and secured a bungalow. The baron had expected at this point to be joined by two of the company's officers, but they had not made their appearance. On the following morning, as soon as breakfast had been disposed of, he ordered preparations to be made for the start. It was not impossible, he thought, that the officers had gone on by the Bur- racur route, and he would not wait for them.

Sir John's attendants were now three in number, I • , the addition taken at the bungalow being two stout fellows—Mark Vining and Adam Cook—who had been brought up at Birchmear, and who were warmly attached to their master. At home they had served chiefly in the stables and in the forest, being expert horsemen and accomplished huntsmen, and his lordship had brought them with him to India because he knew they would be just the at- tendants required upon expeditions like unto that In which he was now engaged. At Nuddya, which they reached on the evening of the second day, Sir John was advised by the Jemmadar, or native chief, not to go on without a stronger force. He said that a large number of Ghnrka soldiers had deserted from Nepaul, and were committing all sorts of depredations in the country to the north. They were worse than the Thugs, he declared. But his story did not sound plausible; and as Sir John was strongly inclined to believe that the fellow only sought to urge his stay in the village that he might tax him heavily for accommodations, he resolved to push on. He here secured two Afghan guides, however, who were well acquainted with the roads, and who appeared to be entirely trustworthy. Towards noon on the third day our party entered the great jungle of Nagdapoor, through which the road held its course for a distance of ten miles or more. Half the dismal solitude had been left behind them when the guides, who rode in advance, suddenly pulled up, uttering wild exclamations of terror as they did so. Sir John hastened to the spot, and beheld by the wayside the bodies of two men, dead, and shockingly mangled. One of the Afghans declared that he recognized them as two native merchants of Nagore who had passed through Nuddya only the day before. And this story seemed reasonable from the fact that the men could not have been dead many hours, as the condition of the bloodpools plainly indicated. It was a horrible sight, and Sir John had no disposition to linger upon it. He had given direc- tions for moving on, when the horse of one of the guides reared with a wild snort, and pushed backward upon the others; and in a moment more a crashing was heard in the thick tangle of the jungle. " The Ghurkas I The Ghurkas !" cried the foremost Afghan, with a terrified shriek; and he would have fled at the top of his speed had not the way been blocked by half a score of dark forms which had darted out into the road.

They came out in front, and they came out in the rear—full a score of them—stout, dusky men, with Tartar features, wearing turbans, and armed with swords and spears. There was blood upon their filthy garments, and it was plain to be seen that they were eager for a renewal of the dreadful bap- tism. They brandished their swords in the air, *ad withiouddlendish yells called upon the travel-

rant,o disni6vilit; -at the-same Mae totaling a ootdoi3 of spears through which the hor oes oould sto. break.

WARRINGTON RUGBY FOOTBALL CLUB MEETING.

THE NORTHERN UNION SUPPORTED.

A meeting of the members of the Warrington Rugby Football Club was held at the 'Norton Arms Hotel Wilderspool Causeway on Thursday week.to consider the action of the committee in joining the North, ru Rugby Union. There was a very large attendance of members. Mr. H. Ashton was voted to the chair unanimously.

The CHAIRX•I4 in opening the meeting, said that the committee had been compelled to take action at once with regard to the formation of the Northern Union. It was bomidered by them that it would be better to join, and it was mow for the meeting to c3nfirm or otherwise their action.

Before any proposition was made,Mr.S.W.Jeffery (the hon. assistant secretary) read the following letter from Mr. J. E. Warren, the hen. sec. of the club, which was addressed to the Chairman :—"Dear Sir,—lt is very un- fortunate that a most important sub-committee meeting in connection with the Union prevents me attending our meeting to-night. I should have liked to have been pre- sent to give a complete history of the circun,stances ' which have led up to the present crisis in the Rugby football world, and to defend the action of our commit- tee in Joining the clubs forming the Northern Union. It is well known that Northern clubs have been recouping their players for loss of wages sus- tained when losing time to play their club matches for years, contrary to the rules of the Rngbv 'Union. There was a strong feeling that the workingmen who chiefly compose the northern clubs shotila not be deprived ,of their sport because they could not afford to lose the time necessary to play the club matches. Some years ago, a determined effort was made to get these payments legalized by the governing body. -rhp movement had the support of the Lancashire and York-hire unions, and [may say that the W.F.O at the time supported very strongly the desired alterations in •the roles. The movement failed owing to the determined .opposition of the southern clubs, the majority of which are composed of players to whom loss of time does not -necessarily mean loss of wages. At the last Rugby Union general meeting, the• Clubs were givento understand that a determined effort would be made to stamp out these ill-gal, but to Northernclubs, necessary payments, and new and more stringent rules were to be framed with this object in view. A meeting of Lancasiaire and York- shire clubs was held in Manchester soon after the Rugby Union meeting to consider the position. It was felt that payment for broken time was absolutely necessary for the successful carryingmn of the game in the north, and an attempt was made to form a combination of clubs with the object of 'being in readiness to supp irt each other in the event of the Union expelling any club for a frivolous offmce against these new professional rules, broken time payments being included under this head. The Rugby Union prohibited the Formation of such a combination and several leading clubs refusing to go as far as a breach with the present ho iv, the movement at the time collapsed. The com- mittee of the Warrington club I may say strongly sup- ported the movement. It was only on the23rd August, that I received a notice to attend a meeting of nine Lancashire clubs at Manchester on the frillowing Tuesday, and a meeting of Lancashire and Yorkshire at Huddersfield on the following Thurs- day. There was not time to convene a general meeting. Prompt action was absolutely necessary, and your com- mittee While fully recognising the many disadvantage which the new 'Union will labour under, decided, after very grave and careful consideration, to throw in their lot with the other clubs. Indeed there appeared to be on alternative. The season was jost opening, and we 'Mould have been left with about 24 open 'dates which It would have been impossible to refill if we had hold aloof. THE RESIGNATION OF I'HE CHIEF CONSTABLE.

In view of the resignation of the Chief Constable- ship of Warrington by Mr. Samuel Hunt, the fol- lowing details of Mr. Hunt's long connection with the police force of the town will be read with in- terest. Mr. Hunt joined the Warrington police force on December 21st, 1863, as superintendent, having left the Derby borough force for that pur- pose. In Derby he had given a service of 13 years and 8 months, and brought with him to Warrington first class testimonials as to character and ability for the whole of that time. In Warrington he has served thirty-one years and eight months, so that his continuous service amounts in all to forty-five years and four months. When Mr. Hunt joined the Warrington police force, Mr. Mac Michael was Chief Constable. This gentleman was anointed to the office in December, 1852, and resigned in July, 1866, and on August 9th following, Mr. Hunt was ap- pointed to the position at a salary of .£145 per annum. The salary has been advanced at various times till 1888 when it reached £3OO per annum, at which It remains to the present day. The strength of the police force when he joined was 16, and the population about 26,000. Now, the force is 54, all told, and the population about 57.000. During the year 1866, a gang of burglars were captured and the proceeds of no fewer than 18 burglaries by them from Warrington, and other places, Lanca- shire or Cheshire, were recovered and restored to the owners, the thieves being sent for trial and convicted. About the same time a Fenian scare took place and it entailed a vast amount of anxiety and labour on the part of the police by night and day. A great number of special constables were placed upon duty along with the regular policemen for the safety of public buildings and the public generally. In the follow- ing year a terrible railway collision at Walton Arches on the London and North Western Railway near Warrington occurred whereby a great number of persons were killed and injured. Inquests on the dead lasted for weeks and nursing and care of the injured for months. During Mr. Hunt's lengthened service there have been seven Parliamentary elec- tions in Warrington and the disturbances which oc- curred at some of these caused the police to have lively times, but no serious damage to property or injury to the person ever resulted. Considering that Warrington is the great highway between 13 other towns with greater populations within a radius of 30 miles and that its own population is increasing rapidly it is and has been singularly free from serious crime and it is claimed that drunken- ness is decreasing gradually but surely.

===== 0002601_18950914_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EMPEROR'S FORK.

The German Emperor was staying some time ago in one of the towns on the shore of the Mediterranean, and as he holds the rank of Admiral in our Navy he was invited to dinner by the commander of the British squadron, then cruising near at hand, says the " Mascot." But when the time for the banquet arrived it was discovered that the important implement was missing. Where was the Raiser's knife and fork ? For though the Emperor can wield sceptre, sword, end pen, he cannot use an or3inary knife and fork. His left arm is practically ('salsas, and he employs an ingenious con- trivance which combines both knife and fork, and does very well with it. This Imperial implement had not been placed beside the Imperial plate,and it could not le found ; not even the Emperor's attendants knew where it was. A picked crew was sent ashore in haate, but though the Kaiser's quarters were searched, no knife and fork could be discovered. All this time the elaborate dinner was spoiling. It was now two hours since the tible had lacked only the Kaiser's knife and fork. But the dinner could not be eaten until the Emperor was ready to eat, and his eating implement must be found. No one had ventured to ask the Kaiser if he knew the whereabouts of the missing article, but hunger rendered the host bold. With decorous delicacy, His Majesty was approached, and the momentous question asked, "could he tell where his knife and fork could he found "Oh, yes •, here it is," said he, as be produced it from an inner pocket of his uniform !

SIX PLITTOCRAIS.

THE WORLD'S RICHEST MEN. According to " As You Like It," the six richest men in the world are vroh Qua, John D. Rockefeller, the Dukeof Westminster, Colonel North, Cornelius Vanderbilt, and Li Flung Chang. The fizures are :—Li Flung Chang, £100,000,000; John D. It.ickefeller, £38.000000; the Dus, ot Westminster, £20,000,000; Cl mel North, £20,000,000; Cornelius Vanderbilt, £20,000,000; Woh QUA, £20,000,000. But two of these mitlionaires inherited their fortunes. One is the Duke of Westminster, the other is Cornelius Vanderbilt. Li Hung Chang alone is in danger of losing his, but then his head might go, too, and a person without that necessary ornament wouldn't enjoy even a biscuit. As Viceroy of the Chinese Empire he was for for years in a position to accumulate wealth of every sort. With his one hundred millions he is the owner of great rice fields and innumerable pawnshops, which are most profitable. Colonel North, at the age of four- teen could not read or write. Some time he served as a sailor. At the age of twenty-three he went to Chili, raised capital, secured command of the nitrate beds, arranged for a water supply, and built railways, gas works, and so on. The Duke of Westminster's income is estimated at from five to seven hundred thousand pounds a year. The vast property he owns in London was bought by one of his ancestors in the sixteenth cen- tury, when it was only an outlying part of London. Rockefeller's fortune is sup- posed to be over thirty-six million pounds. His father was a physican. All his money had been made out of his oil fields. Cornelius Vanderbilt is probably the thriftiest of the sons of William H., and has actually more money than he knows what to do with. The Van- derbilt money has been inherited,and the system bearing the name, is supposed to be worth near sixty millions,of which tine favoured son owns a third. The wealth of Cornelius is estimated by those who know him inti- mately as £20,000,000. Woh Qua, the great Canton tea merchant has a fortune estimated at twenty millions. For years the trade in tea has been centred in him. Years ago Woh Qua, who had worked himself up in the firm with which he was connected lodged ahead fifty years, and saw the vast possibilities of the business. Every agent paid him a commission. Every pound of tea grown in the Celestial Empire had sooner or later to come to him or his agents. His vessels are numbered by the thousand, and as a matter of fact he commands more ships than the Admiral of any princi- pality. There are, of course, other great individual for- tunes, such as those of Baron Hirsch, the Asters, and the Rothschilds. The two latter, however,are jointly owned by half-a-dozen members of the family, and while the sum itself is great, it would not make each member as rich as either of the six men mentioned above if it were to be apportioned among them.

===== 0002601_18950914_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
—,b._...... FRUIT AS FOOD.

The great mass of the people look on fruit as a luxury upon which they can only spend odd peonies for the amusement of their children. Many parents will, says " Science Siftings," more readily spend money on injur- ious, or even poisonous sweets, than they will on good healthy fruit, and fashionable society will spend pounds on c4kes, wine and brandies, whilst they spend as many shillings on what is of more consequence—truit. The amount of money spent on purgatives and other correc- tives is incalculable and the greater par,: might be saved and advantageously diverted to other channels if fruit was regarded as a necessary article of diet. Why, asks Mr. Benj4field, MB., in an article on the subject in the " Horticultural Review," should we not have fruit palaces as well as gin palaces where fruit might be obtainsble at popular prices ? Eve soon discovered that fruit was good for food. Every generation since has endorsed her opinion, and now, perhaps more than ever before, the world is begin- ning to realise the alimentary value of fruit. Good ripe fruit contains a large amount of sugar in a very easily digestible form. This sugar constitutes a light nourish- ment, which, in conjunction with bread, rice, etc., forms a food especially suitable fer warm climates. When eaten with, say milk or milk and eggs, the whole forms the most perfect and easily digestible foad imaginable. For stomachs capable of -digesting it, fruit eaten with I pastry composes very perfect nourishment. Probably, however, most people would prefer to take their cooked fruit covered with rice and milk or cultard.

Fruit is not a complete dietary in itself, but it is an excellent adjunct to a meat dietary. The acids it con- tains assist digestion, and it is for this reason we eat apple sauce with our roas6 goose and pork, the fat of which it renders assimilable, and hence we consume these delicacies with greater enjoyment and relish. It was instinct first taught men to do this; to-day science supplies the reason and further tells Us that fruit should be eaten as an aid to digestion of other foods much more than it is now. Cultivated fruits, such as apples pears, cherries, strawberries, grapes, &c., contain n o analysis very similar proportions of the same ingre- dients, which are about 8 per cent, of grape sugar,3 per cent, of pectine, 1 per cent, of flash-forming albumenoids with over 80 per cent, of water. Digestion upon the action of pepsine in the stomach upon the food, which is greatly aided by the acids of the stomach. Fats are digested by these acids, and the bile from the liver. Now the acids and the pectine in fruit peculiarly assist the acids of the stomach. Lemon juice has been prescribed largely by physicians to help weak digestion, simply be- cause these acids exist very abundantly in the lemon. . . . . . . .

The antiscorbutic action of certain fruits is well-known and sailors at sea would be badly off indeed were an abundant supply of vegetables and fruit net provided for their coasumption. Good fruit clears the blood and promotes health. As a medicine, fruit is a most valuable !Illy. As previously shown, when the body is in that breaking-up condition known as scurvy the whole medical profession look upon frait and fresh vegetables as the one and only known remedy. Mr. Benjafield believes "the day will COMP when science will use it very ranch more largely than it does now in the treatment of many of the every-day ailments. We have shown how it aids digestion. Observations in scurvy prove that it exerts a very powerful influence on the blond. And ' the blood is the life ' ; poor blood means poor spirits, poor strength, poor breath, and poor circulation.

"Impure blood means gout,rheurnatisna,skin diseases, ricketts, and other troubles. As it is proved that fruit will purify and improve the quality of the Wood,it must follow that fruit is both food and medicine combined. In fevers, grapes and strawbeiries may be used with advantage when given to the patient in small but fre- quent doses : oranges and baked apples if the others are not obtainable. For rheumatism, plenty of lemons are invaluable. White girls with miserable pallid com- plexions want a quart of strawberries a day ; where these are not obtainable, bananas, which contain much iron, are a good substitute. _

"Probably, of all fruits, the apple stands unrivalled for general purposes in the household, either raw or cooked it can be taken by nearly everybody, and it contains similar properties to the other more delicate fruits. The pear is, perhaps, more easily digested than the apole, and for eating uncooked is superior to it." Dried fruits are becoming more popul ir with our colonial friends. This is not surprising. They serve as an excellent substitute where fresh truit is unohtainable. They have been found effective safeguards, Mr. Benja- Bold assures us, in keeping off scurvy from our colonial troops. Dried fruits on account of their cheapness might be more largely patronised by our own working classes, as they are cheap and are always in season. For preserving fruit, Mr. Benjafield thinks bottling in glass will be the practice in future. Not by the use of such dangerous chemicals as salycilic and boracic acids, but simply by protecting it, after cooking.frorn the fer- mentive germs in the atmosphere. Tinned fruit is not so good ; the acid of the fruit dissolves tin and lead, and vary serious cases of illness sometimes follow. Besides, fruit could be said much cheaper in bottles than in tins, as the bottle can be returned and used again. . Jams made from nice fresh fruit, and put up in glass or ware, make a very good article of diet, but much of the jams of commerce should be used as food for pigs. Jams act on tin and lead very much like tart fruits, but the acid in them is greatly neutralised by the sugar. Still, the outside of the jam in a tin is often quite dis- coloured.

A medical writer has recently been maintaining that bread and other starchy food,containing as they do large quantities of lime, are responsible, especially in aged people, for many of the diseases from which we suffer, such as apsoplexy, rheumatic gout, etc., and has urged that fruit should be taken freely instead, to counteract these limy effects. One of the first symptoms, when people are deprived of fruit and vegetables, is very severe pain in the side like rheumastism, and death from failure of the heart's action. Whether he is right about this lime may not be proved, but there is no doubt hut lime exists too largely in the blood vessAs in these dis- eases, and if fruit were eaten regularly it would do much to prevent it. Science to-day tells us that we may live uuder the most beautiful conditions, we may feast on bread, eggs, rice, cocoa, oatmeal, and such like foods for a short time, hut unless we take fruits or fresh vegetables— fruits being the best—we shall get listless, with leaden face, etc., until we die in a few months at the longest, and it follows that if we would keep ourselves and our children with clear skins, bright intellects, good diges- tion, rich colour, healthy blood, and strength for work, we must regularly take fruit and vegetables and look upon them as actually more necessary for the support of good health than any other article of diet.

IRELAND TO TO MEXICO.

The Mexican Government,aceorling to the New York " Morning Journal," offers to giVO to all Irishmen who will agree to go to the country not only all the land that they can cultivate, hut will defray all their expenses from the time they leays Ireland until they are landed in the Republic of Mexico. A special appropriation has been made to defray the expenses of a commissioner to Ireland. Senor Matias RoMero,the Mexican Minister in Washington, has been informed of the wishes of his Gwernment, and has been ordered to look around and find a suitable commissioner to undertake the work of stirring the Irish up to a real- isation of the new proanerity that awaits them under the balmy skies of the Mexican Republic. Minister Romero was in New York last week, and had a long consultation with some of the leaders of the Irish patriotic movement in that city,

The intention of the Mexican Government in this matter has been brought formally to the attention of the new English Prime Minister. This is a courtesy that always obtains among nations. If England objects to a Mexican representative going publicly through Ireland and picking out the cream of the country for settlers in the great republic of the tropics, measures equally effec- tive will, says the American paper, be taken to let the Irish paopla know of the opportunities that await them in this land of promise. It is confidently expected that the commissioner to be appointed tw the Mexican Government will leave for his post within two weeks. He is to be an American who ig familiar with the natural advantages of Mexico. He will have unusual powers given him and practically un- limited capital behind him. By next winter there will be at least 300 Irish families transplanted from the Green Isle to the Southern Republic. Consul-General Navarro says the Irish people who went to Mexico years ago have made a great deal of money. From farming they drifted into business life and most of their sons are now in the learned profes- sions.

NOlk S ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

FOOTBALL.

The Rugby football season was opened on Saturday, thanks to the fetmation of the Northern Union. Other- wise, the first match would have been fought next Saturday. There was a vast amount of interest in the match on Saturday, as Warrington's opponents were Hunslet, a team who are verc prominent among the Yorlohire clubs, as in the Yorkshire League last year, they finished fifth with 24 points, and are also noted cup fighters. Consequently,a very large "gate "assembled, and as the game turned out, the spectators certainly had their money's worth, for despite the heat and the want of practice, it was of a most attractive and lively description. If such games are played every week,there need be no fear of poor "gates."

Warrington have every reason to be satisfied with themselves on the occasion of their debut in the Northern League competition. They have begun with a win. True, it is a very narrow one, but still it is sufficieet to give them two points on the table. They are now tenth on the table, out of the 22 clubs. Last season, excluding their holiday match with Mona, they had played four matches before they had won their first victory and that was won only by six points to five. Their programme this season is a long way ahead of that of last season, but judging from their performance on Saturday, they will give a very good account of themselves, and will take some beating.

The result of live points to four in favour of Warring- toc fairly represents the superiority of the Warrington team over Hunslet. There was not much bet seen them, but the former did a little more pressing than their opponents. Each side had very determined set of for- wards, and among the Hunslet men, Walsh was especi- ally prominent. The Warrington set worked extremely hard, and none more so than Turner. Fair. Turner has been fairly described as the " evergreen," and on Satur- day his display was equal to that of his best days. He along with Taylor, Sankey and Nevins formed the pick of a hard working lot. The" old uns" are apparently not done with yet.

Foden and Bate played well at half and the former had the honour of opening the scoring for Warrington dur- ing the present season. Their opponents also worked with a will, Gillings being the best of the two. The three-quarter line has been much improved by the in- clusion of O'Brien,and Barber,Burton,O'Brien,and Carey form a strong three-quarter line, but new that Broady is available, it will be still stronger. Boscow and Gold- thorpe were two backs who were exceptionally safe, but Boscow had the advantage of Goldthorpe in being able to use either foot. Goldthorpe bad more speed and the ' dropped goal he scored was a beauty, and was only equalled by the placed goal which Burton kicked for Warrington. But for the two full backs, the scoring would have been much more frequent, and the fact that only one score was made on either side is very creditable to both of them. The Warrington lines have not yet been crossed even to the extent of a minor, and this speaks well for the defence.

Hull is taken on to•day, and if Warrington can beat Hunslet, surely they can beat Hull, for whereas Huns- let were fifth, Hull tied with Wakefield Trinity for the bottom place in the Yorkshire league. This however must not make Warrington over confident, for most of the defeats which Hull suffered last year were only narrow ones, and they beat the only Lancashire club (Rochdale Hornets) they met both at home and away— at home to the extent of four goals to a try. A eood game should therefore be seen, and also a good " gate."

To do battle for Warrington to-day, the following team has been chosen; Boscow, full back; Barber, O'Brien, Burton, and Broady, three-quarters; Bate and Foden, half backs; Turner, Dakin, Berry, Bucaley,Swift, Sankey, Nevins, and Taylor, forwards.

There were ten games played in the Northern League on Saturday, all the clubs being engaged with the excep- tion of Oldham and Huddersfield. Of the ten games, six were won by the home clubs,and four by the visitors. In three of the matches, Lancashire and Yorkshire clubs were pitted against each other, and two of Lan- cashire, Warrington and Tyldeslev were successful over Hunslet and Manningham, while Leeds got the better of Leigh. Brighouse Rangers, a Yorkshire club, also de- feated Stockport. a Cheshire club. It must be admitted however, that the matches between Lancashire and Yorkshire clubs were all fought in Lancashire. The highest score on Saturday was that of Runcorn, who beat their neighbours " over the water" by 15 points to 4, but the most decisive victory was by Bradford who won by 11 points to nil. Warrington scored the nar- rowest victory.

There are 22 clubs in the Northern League, and these clubs besides forming one large league, also have two separate leagues one composed of Yorkshire clubs, and the other of Lancashire and Cheshire clubs. There- fore, whenever a Yorkshire club plays another Yorkshire club the match counts both in the Northern and York- shire league, and the same with the Lancashire and Cheshire matches.

During the seven days ending Friday the 6th Septem- ber 152 seaeoing vessels, with a total net registered totinage of 54,862 tons entered and cleared from the post of Manchester, which includes all parts of the Ship Canal. This is an increase of 10.895 tons on the returns for t e prec.-ding period of seven days, although the vessels navigating the waterway were fewer by 28, the figures for the previ'.us week being 180 ships of 43,98? net registe,ed tons. They thus swell the coastwise arrivals and the foreign departures.

'FAIIICLOUGII v. SHIP CANAL COMPANY.

THE RECENT ARBITRATION. " The decision of Mr. Harrison Raster in the case of Fairclough v. the Manchester Ship Canal Company is, says the " Manchester Guardian,"" one of great impor- tance to the shareholders. It is in favour of the Com- pany on all points, and it gives relief from one of those "onerous" forms of obligati m which have so often been noted. By one of those clauses in the Canal Company's Act imposed under direst necessity upon the promoters of the Canal, it was laid upon them to prove, if at any time after the commencement of their works the naviga- tion betweeir Widnes and Liverpool should become im- paired, that the injury was not caused by anything they bad done. In opening the case for the complainants council triumphantly and confidently declared the im- possibility of proving this neeltive. The circumstances A the case were these : In 1885 or 1888 Messrs. Fair- dough purchased a wharf at Widnes and built thereon a mill and warehouse. There is no doubt that whee they bought this wharf—known as Cooper's Wharf—it was accessible to vessels such as they proposed to use in conveying graie to and from Widnes and Liver- pool or Warrington. Mere is equally little doubt that after they built the wharf became less easily accessible by reason of a mudbank forming in front. The point to be decided by the arbitrator was,Had this mudbank been formed by reason of something done in carrying out the Ship Canal works ? In the minds of Messrs. Fairclough and their advisers it was simply a case of post hoc erg* pro pter hoc—the one fact had followed the other, and therefore it was assumed to have been caused by it.Thev endeavoured to show that three things done by the Cana Company might have singly or together caused the mis- chief. They had excavated some rocks in the Widnes channel known as the Snig Pie Rocks, and this had re- moved an ancient swirl of the tide which had formerly scoured the frontage of Cooper's Wharf. They had built a concrete wall along the Cheshire bank of the river to divide the river from the Canal This might have had the effect of drawing the deep- water channel fromi the Widnes to the Cheshire share. But by far the greatest importance was attached to the contention that the fresh water from the upper reaches of the river had been diverted through the Lstchford locks from its former course, and had consequently ceased to wash away any slight deposits which in former i times might have lodged n front of Cooper's W harLand- thus this bank had been allowed to accumulate until it had almost entirely impeded access to the wharf. These contentions, most ably urged by Mr. Walton-Lawson, Q.C., and Mr. Hughes, were supported by scientific witness of eminence and by Mr. Richard Fairclough and other practical men. The defence of the Company, in the hands of Mr. Balfour Brown, Q C., and Mr. J. F. Byrne, was that this mudbank had constantly forered from time to time, to a greater degree no doubt in some years than others, and long before Faircloughs had ac- quired Cooper's Wharf had occasionally been removed by artificial means. 7he former occu- pant of the wharf, Mr. Gandy, testified that the bank had frequently formed during his occu- pation. Another witness testified that he had been em- ployed in former years to clear the bank with a huge rake drawn by ropes attached to a locomotive engine. It was also proved that the Sankey brook was formerly diverted by some fixed erection termed a" stank " 80 as to wash away the upper portion of this mudbank directly above the wharf. As to the diversion of the main channel, it was proved by the charts of the Mer- sey Conservancy taken during thirty years that the chanoel had wandered about the estuary in the mast fit- ful and unaccountable manner, sometimes hugging the Laneashire, sometimes the Cheshire shore, and some- times meandering in every conceivable intermediate course. Sir Leader Williams,Sir Benjamin Baker,Mr.G. H. Hill, Mr. Vernon Harcourt, and other witnesses testi- fied that the removal of the Snig Pie Rocks and the building of the Canal wall along the Cheshire bank could have had no possible practical effect in producing a mudbank at this particular spot or withdrawing the deep water channel across the bed of the estuary. But, strong as was this evidence of the hol- howness of the claim set up,the final touch was given to the case by Mr. Balfour Brown in dealing with the strong point of the complainants—the abstrac- tion of water. This was done by reading one of the complainant's letters dated in June, 1891, which set forth the injury then sustained, the depth of mud in front of the wharf, the consequent difficulty of access—in fact, all that was alleged as the cause of action. It was per- fectly clear that all the harm done, if any, had been done before that date, but, unfortunately for the com- plainant's case, no water was diverted through the Latchford locks and Ship Canal before the following November, when, it will be remembered, a great flood broke down the barriers and the intended diversion of the water was anticipated. Under the circumstances, and in face of the evidence, it is difficult to see how the arbitrator could have come to any other decision than that arrived at, and it is doubtful whether, if the obli- gation had rested in the ordinary way upon the cora- plaiaant to prove his case, the action would ever have been brought. Had Messrs. Fairclough succeeded, the result would have been calamitous to the Canal. The remedy provided in the Act, if the Company had been found liable, involves the dredging of a deep-water channel to one or other of their locks and keep- ing this open at all times, the free use of the Canal as an alternative waterway, and free towage through the Canal to Eastham ; and if the decision had ben adverse to the Company it would have been liable to an indefinite number of claims from all who now or at any future time should possess wharves in these lower reaches of the river. The case occupied eight days in hearing, and from the number and eminence of the witnesses on either side and the counsel engaged it must have involved very great expense. It is to be hoped that the result may deter further attempts to extort ad- vantage by reason of terms, inequitable in themselves* which ought never to have been inserted in the Act, and never would have been but for the Otero ~tive being pre- sented—" accept this clause or lose the c sts of a Parlia- mentare campaign." ^ --------

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

AN OLD OFFENDER.

On Tuesday morning before &Peers. F. J. Norman and E. Imison, at Runcorn Police Court, an elderly man named James Mc rtirk was charged with indecent con- uct on the previous day. Inspector Hicks said the man Ad some 20 convictions recorded against him. A fine o . and ss. 4th costa was imposed or a month's in- pr ,onment.

===== 0002601_18950914_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
EMPLOYEES' PreNic.

employes oi Mr. Walter Collier, grocer, held their qixtt annual excursion on Wednesday. The party, numbering about 40 went in two waggonettes to Chester where they visited the places of interest, and also went to Eccleston Ferry, and Eaton Hall. They were enter- tained to dinner by Mr. Collier, who also defrayed all other expenses.

===== 0002601_18950914_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
GRE-ENWAY-ROAD PRIMITIVE METHODIST SCHOOL.

The members of the girls " beehive " in connection with this school had their first annual outing on Wednes- day. The juveniles were conveyed on a lurry kindly lent by Mr. Ackerley to Clifton, where they indulged in games on the land of Mr. John Rowlands. This gentle- men also permitted them to enjoy themselves as they pleased in his orchard. The party returned at 7.30. The arrangements were under the superintendence of Misses florworth, Lunt, Shingler and Elillidge.

THEFT OF POTATOES AT CLIFTON.

At Runcorn Police Court, on Thursday morning, an elderly man named George Robinson, of no fixed anode, was brought up before Messrs. Speakman and Imison, on a charge of stealing 31bs. of potatoes, growing in a field b.longing to William Griffiths, of Clifton, on the previrus day. The prisoner was caught in the act by PC. Paveraft, stationed at Weston Point. Mr. Griffiths informed the Bench that considerable damage had been done to his potatoes and turnips by persons trespassing. default of paying a fine of ss. and costs, the man man was sent to gaol for seven days.

• PERILOUS POSITION OF A RUNCORN ROL

On Tuesday morning, about 11 o'clock, Charles Edwards, the son of Captain Edwards, of the John and Henry, laden with stone, had, to say the least, a very unpleasant experience whilst the vessel was in the lock. Close to the Francis basin, Runcorn docks, he tried to jump ashore from the boat. He, however, miAjuriged the distance, and fell into the water, narrowly escaping being crushed between the vessel and the wall. The ma'e succeeded in quickly rescuing him from his peril- qus p isition, when it was found he was little the worse for his immersion.

THE RUNCORN BRIDGE CHARGE.

A meeting of the Parliamentary and General Purposes Co-nmittee of the Runcorn District Council was held on Wednesday evening. The proposition recently intro- duced by the Rev. Dr. Gastaldi with regard to approach- ing the L. and N.-W.R. Company respecting the fares charged for crossing Runcorn bridge, etc. was considered. During the discussion and after the question was carefully considered, it was decided not tt, take the proposed action, until other subjects which are still under negotiation are settled. The question with regard to the holding of committee meetings every month was also broached,and it was resolved that if, in the opinion of the chairman, the vice-chairman, or two members, there was any busi- ness requiring the attention of the committee, the com- mittee shall be called together.

RUNCORN TONIC SOL-FA PRIZE CHOIR.

- The adjourned general meeting of the mpmbns of this choir was held on Friday evening in Ellesmere-street schoolroom, under the presidency of Mr. S. Coventry. The completion of the election of officers and committee shows the following result :—Coaductor, Mr. John Flolford ; chairman, Mr. Hugh Hughes; librarian, Mr. Jseell Brown ; secretaries, Messrs. G. W. Yates and R. W, Newell ; treasurer, Mr. W. Coventry ; committee, Mrs. Stubbs, Mrs. Gandy and Mrs. Leathwood, Misses E iwards, Davies, Pope, Littler and Williams ; Messrs. Worrdll, Clare, Pritchard, .Kniveton, Shaw, Newell, Flarrison and Pendlebury. It was resolved that a printed b-ilance sheet be issued every season, giving full particu- lars of the choir's working,and also that the forthcoming season be opened with a gentlemen's "at home."

RUNCORN SONS OF TEMPERANCE.

At the ordinary meeting of the above association, on Saturday, under the presidency of Mr. Ernest Hobson, Mr. Ed ward Key referred to the present condftion of the juvenile temperance societies, and the importance of making the meetings interesting and entertaining. After this a conversation took place in which the following officers took part: Messrs. David Smith, Thomas Clarke, John Jones, Thomas J. Holt, Thomas Cooke and Daniel Bisbrowne. It was stated the work of training the young in temperance principles could not be over-estim- ated, and that the meetings should be made as attractive as possible. The Secretary reported that there were 78 members of the cadet section, which was deemed sa:is- factory. The boys are now competing for a prize offered by Mr. Thomas Cooke.

TRH LATH _MRS. BAZLEY

Relative to the death of Mra.Bazlev,wife of Mr. Joseph Bazley, of Vine-street, an In Memoriam. Service was con- ducted on Sunday evening in the Camden Chapel, Run- corn, by Mr. P. Sp,,akman, J.P., C.C., in the presence of a large congregation. Several appropriate hymns were sung by the choir, assisted by an orchestra. Miss S. Blzley sang" When peace like a river." Miss Temper- ley gave an effective rendition of What is this that steals upon my frame," and the choir, under the leader- ship of Mr. S. Bazley and W.Tickle, sang" Vital Spark." Mr. J. Temperley presided at the organ. In the course of an address, Mr. Speakman spoke ot the life of a Christian. He alluded to Mrs. Bazley, whom he had known for many years as one who had lived the life of a consistent Christian, and he trusted that her example would be followed by those connected with Camden Church. He urged the young to hold dear the traditions nf Christian fathers and mothers, and appealed to the Church to labour with renewed vigour in the further- ance of the Master's cause.
