RUNCORN PARISH CHURCH ORGAN RECITAL.

On Sunday, an organ recital was given after evensong by Mr. B. Jackson, F.C.C., organist of the People's Palace, London. Mr. Jackson who had given the organ recitals a St. George's Hall, Liverpool, on the previous day and was the guest of Mr. Johnson, organist of the Itunc,,rn Parish Church kindly consented to give a short recital and submitted the following pieces which were attentively listened to by a large congregation : " Meditation "in D major, B. Jackson ; variations in tune to " All glory, laud and honour," B. Jackson; Berceue, Delbanck ; grand chorus in D major, A. Guil- mont. The manner in which the various pieces were played testified Mr. Jackson as an organist of exceptional ability,and it is not too much to say that the remarkable powers exhibited by him on Sunday evening created a very earnest and natural desire among many members of the con gregation that they may again have the pleasure and privilege of listening to him.

HOLY TRINITY CHURCH HARVEST THANKSGIVING.

The Holy Trinity Church harvest Thanksgiving ser- vices were commenced on Wednesday evening, when the sermon was preached by the Rev. Canon Rycroft, D.D., to a large congregation. An anthem was also sung by the choir entitled "The eyes of all wait upon thee, 0 Lord," the solo part being taken by Miss F. Bradshaw. The church was beautifully decorated with cereals, fruits, flowers, &c., a large number of which had been contributed by residents in the district. The following, sent flowers and fruit : Messrs. Salkeld, Fisher, Horsefield, Hankinson, Weatbrook, W. Howard, John Orme, J. Owen, and A. Savage; and Mesdames Meadowcroft, Waterworth, Warburton, Corker,Street, Robinson, Strin- ger, Grieve, Marislow, West, Harrison, Ackerley, and Grifiths ; Misses Bradshaw, Hitchmough, and Coleburn, and Masters Reed and Joyce. The following assisted in the decorating :—Miss Preston, Mrs. West, Westbrook, Grieve, Misses Tyne, Horsefield. Lea, Warner, E. Turner, Ilankinson, Master T. Bradshaw. Mr. Grieve kindly lent a number of plants. HOLY TRINITY SCHOOLS, RUNCORN. The annual treat of the Trinity Church Sunday Schools was held on Friday. There was a large gather. tug to the number of 250. These were under the leader- ship of the Vicar, Dr. Preston, and Mrs. Preston, who were assisted by Messrs. Westbrook and D. Smith and Miss Preston and Miss Tyrie. The party assembled at Trinity schoolroom and headed by the drum and file band, under the bandmaster, Mr. Jones, marched in orderly procession to the railway station, wuere they embarked in engaged carriages and 'proceeded to the Overton Hills. There they enjoyed themselves in mani- fold diversions, and had a most excellent tea which was provided by Mrs. Parker. They were joined at this by the warden, Mr, Hapkinson. The return journey was made without mishap ; and all reached borne the better for their" out." On Saturday the infants had their treat in a field, kindlyfent by Mr. flaziehurst. Berea swing attached to a new and strong wooden frame,made and erected by the kindness of Mr. D. Smith, was the chief object of attraction. The little ones thoroughly en- joyed themselves, and I eturned to the schoolroom,where a good tea, which had been brewed by Mr. Bates, was served out. The teachers present with the infants were Misses Preston, Dobey, Rigby, Horsefield, Annett, and McLeod. The Vicar and Messrs. Westbrook and Lt. Smith were also present.

NELSON-STREET SUNDAY SCHOOL. On Sunday a pleasant evening was spent at the above school, Mr. Alfred Harrison in chair. The affair was arranged by the officials and teachers of the school to start the subscription list for attaining uniforms for the bielson-street School Subscription Band. The place was made bright with bunting, &c. In the room on the ground floor was one large table, well laden with fruit, cakes, jelly, &c„ given by friends. Upstairs one of the class rooms was decorated and used for a tea room. The first part of the programme consisted of items given by the concert party connected with the band. Mr. J. Foulkes was the accompanist. After the concert the friends partook of the good things provided, the novelty being that everything was a penny, !a charge of which none complained. Tne arrangements were in hand of a committee,with Mr. J.L.Littlemore as chairman,and the Misses M. Okell and Stringer as secretaries. The promo- ters considered themselves well repaid for their troubl.• when the amount made was announced to to be £6 55. ld, which will be put to the uniform fund. The usual vote of thanks to the chairman and workers brought a very enjoyable evening toe close.

THE EXAMINER, SATURPAY, SEPTEMBER 14, 1895.

Sr. MICHAEL'S CHURCH, RUNCORN.

The annual sermons on behalf of the Sunday school were preached in this church on Sunday. In the morn- ing the Rev. Canon Wood occupied the pulpit. In the afternoon a children's service was conducted, when an address was delivered by the Rev. H. C. Moyle, The evening preacher was the Rev. R M. Ainslie, M.A. vicar of St. Saviour's, Liverpool. A special feature in connection with the services was the singing of hymns by the scholnrs, who had been carefully trained by Mr. Charles Powna!l,the conductor of the choir. The collec- tions,abich were on behalf of the school funds,realised a satisfactory amount. RUNCORN ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL CLUB.

A general meeting of the members of this club was heol on Wednesday evening, for the purpose of appoint- ing a captain and vice-captain for the reserve team. Mr. G. H. Grounds was voted to the chair. The nomina- tions for the respective offices were as follows : Captain : F. Burrows, C. Burrows, E. Gleave, J. Parry, R. Green, J.Clarke. Vice-captain : G. Casson, J.Clarke, E. Gleave, C. Burrows, J. Parry, R. Green, G. F. Bell. Voting by ballot having been taken the Chairman de- clared that F. Burrows had been duly appointed captain of the reserve team, and J. Parry vice-captain.

===== 0002601_18950914_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN CO-OPERLTIVE SOCIETY.

An adjourned meeting of the members of this society Was held on Tuesday evening in Lacey-st. schoolroom, Widnes,under the presidency of Mr. James Sayles. There was a good attendance. The rules were further con- sidered and a number of them were passed as printed, while in several others there were a few verbal altera- tions. With regard to the servants of the socie.y having no vote, it was decided that in future those who were members should be entitled to vote for the board of management. As to the payment of subscriptions, it was resolved that the practice hitherto in vogue of pav- ing a penny per month towards defraying the necessary expenses of management should be discontinued. The rule respecting depreciations was altered to read as fol- lows :—" That the depreciation of cottages which the Society may build to hold, and all other property in- cluding offices and rolling stock be at the rate of 5 per cent. per annum." The amount previously was ai per cent, per annum. The meeting was protracted, but the discussion was of a very amicable and business-like character. The rule having reference to the reserve fund will be considered at an adjourned meeting.

===== 0002601_18950914_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
DUTTON SCHOOL BOARD.

At. the recent monthly meeting of the Dutton School Board, the Rev. L D. Bennett presiding, the report on the last school examination was produced and read, of which the following is a cony :—Average for the year, 50; grant £5O, extra £lO, total £6O. Principal grant is 149 , discipline is. 81., singing 61.. English 21., geography 25., Lnd needlework 2s. The summary of the inspector's remarks are as follows :—" The school is in good order, and does exceedingly well. The spelling of the second class falls rather below the other work. The infants are not well provided for. They pass a satisfac- tory examination as infants, though an unusually large number are over seven years of age; but they are taught on a gallery in the same room with the other children and there are no facilities for marching or exercises. If they had a properly qualified teacher tha additional grant (Art 105) might be obtained. The Board should take immediate step!: te provide more suitable accom- modation for the infants than exists at present." The members of the Board were gratified with the good grant earned, as it is the highest grant attainable for a school. The school had also earned the" good " report for drawing.

WESLEYAN TEA MEETING AT RUNCORN.

WELCOMING THE NEW MINISTER.

On Monday evening a circuit tea meeting was held in Brunswick Wesleyan Schoolroom, to welcome the Rev. C. de P. Gliddon. There was a large attendance. The tables were presided over by Mesdames Baguley,Bellamy, Boys, B. Crosby, Dyer, Findlow,Garner, J. B. Houghton, Harrison, T. Johnson, Jones, Lambert, Leadstone, Marshall. Morris, Nixon, F. J. Norman, Peacock, Povah, Rawlinson and R. Yates; Misses Berrington, Chadwick, Dobson, Norman and Temperley. At a later hsur a well-attended meeting was held in St. I aul's Church, under the presidency of Mr. R. Raw- linson. He was supported by the Revs. T. Brackenbury, W. George and C. de P. Gliddon; Messrs. F. J. Norman, J.P., P Speakman, J.P., C.C., John Simpson, T. Simla- ter, B. Kniveton, J. Ball, Looker, Moores and B. Crosby (secretary). After devotional exercises, The Chairman spoke of the difficulties they had experi- enced through the indisposition of the Rev. Lyle Stone. They wrote to Mr. Gliddon, who at once accepted the invitation. They must not expect all from their minis- ters—they were only human; but as office bearers and congregation they had to do their part in the work, and if they did so they would have a very happy and pros- perous year. Proceeding he alluded to the death of Mr. Thomas Handley, J.P., which had been a very severe blow to his fellow townsmen, he being a man of great promise. Mr. John Simpson said they had every reason to believe that the services of their new minister would be attended with success. It was with regret, and under painful cireumstances they had to part with Mr. Stone; and they had a hard fight to get Mr. Gliddon, to whom they gave a very hearty welcome to the circuit. He believed him to be a thoroughly good man, and that his one earn- est desire wag to do an excellent work in the Runcorn circuit (Applause.) Mr. Simister spoke of the pleasure he felt in having 1 the opportimity ei giving their third minister and his wife a hearty welcome, even if it was not in the shape of a fermal vote. The Methodist Church, he said, with it dauffhters, had 23,000.000 adherents, and upwards of 5.000,000 members. (Applause.) To-day they heard a good deal of Apostolic succession, but he believed their ministers were eve in that respect with the Pope of Rome. (Applause.) They were told in the Bible "by their fruits ye shall know them." If men were saving souls, blessing humanity, turning men and women from sin to Christ and goodness, he argued that they were in the Apostolic succession. There was a full representa- tion of the circuit, and he urged them all to do their share in the important work in which they were engaged. (Appian.° ) The Rey. T. Brackenbury said their new minister was a men of reputation, and they, greeted him as a brother in the work of Christ. He considered that as Methodists they required a great deal of external stimulation to per- form the gigantic work placed in their hands. They wished Methodism to get on—that the congregations would increase, that Sunday schools would do their work better, that week night services would be more largely attended, that society classes would have their membership augmented, that house to house visitation would he productive of more fruitful results, that their, mission would prosper, in order that the world might be inftmed with the Gospel of Christ ; and that matters might be improved in the circuit. They wished every one to progress in grace and Christian usefulness, until , they reached their home in Heaven. (Applause.) Mr Speakman, on behalf of the Camden Church,eave Mr. Gliddon a hearty welcome. At Camden they wished to do acme good work. The place was a home, and a happy one ; it was also very much like a beehive, in which a noble work was being accomplished. At the last Sacrament they had 84 communicants, which showed they were growing. They had a good feeling in the class meetings, in the Sunday school, and among the workers, which was very helpful and encouraging. In conclusion, he made a touching reference to those whom they had lost through death, specially mentioning Mr.Temperley and Mrs. B zley, who had been busy workers in the hive for a good many years. Mr. Ball also welcomed Mr.and Mrs. Gliddon on behalf of Weston. The Rev. W. George alluded to his twelve months' stay in the circuit, and to haying had the opportunity of testing the kindly words spoken respecting him at a similar gathering last year. He had proved that they were honest and real. Continuing, he spoke of a living church, saying that it commanded respect, aid conse- quently prosperity. They ought by their actions to show , that they were a living church. They would never go ' ahead as they ought until they bed enthusiastic devotion and if they were to have that, to carry their church to, the position it ought to occupy, they must have devotion founded an respect. He advised the young people to study their church, and see what it had done, so that they would admire and love it, and then determir e to do more to strengthen It. They required intelligent enthu- siasm, and when they accomplished this they would make Methodism a greater power for good. Mr. Looker haying spoken on behalf of Halton, The Rev. C. de P. iffiddon, who was heartily receive, then said he was proud to be associated with such a circuit. Be was a Methodist by conviction and belief, and its history was the most inspiring that could be handed down to men. They had had, and still had, grand preachers, and they had won splendid triumphs of the cross through Metho- dist workers. Scriptural holiness was the purpose for which Christ had raised them up. Proceed- ing, he spoke of the wealth of the Methodist Church, their total income being £80,000,000 a year. Then they were rich in generosity, in being able to give en much to other churches ; they were wealthy in workers, and in the Runcorn circuit they were rich in the young men of their church. That was a wealth for which they must thank God unceasingly. In studying the history of their church, let them adopt the motto "Onward, upward and wider," until they had brought into touch with the cross of Christ all those around them. (Applause.) Mr. Kniveton, on behalf of the church and congrega- tion of Weston Point,gave Mr. Gliddon a hearty wel- come, and expressed a hope that his services would be attended with success. During the evening the united choirs of St. Paul's, Halton-rnad, Camden and Halton, rendered the anthems " And the glory of the Lord" (Flandel's " Messiah ")and 46 I Will feed My flock " (C. Simper), the bass solo of which was suns, by Mr. Thomas Johnson, of the Halton- road choir. The singing was under the conductorship of Mr. R. Morris, and Mr. Grice presided at the organ. Totes of thanks to those who had prepared tea, car- ried out the arrangements, and taken part in the sing- ing closed the meeting.

DEATH OF MR. T. HANDLEY, J.P., It

The news of the unexpected death of Mr. Thomas Handley, S.F., of Ferncliffe, Higher Runcorn, will be re- ceived with widespread regret. The sad event took place about three o'clock on Saturday afternoon, after a brief and painful illness (inflimmation of the veins). Mr. Handley MO been danger, msly ill the previous week but hopes were entertained of his recovery. He was, in the opinion of all, making satisfactory progress when, at the time stated, he suddenly expired. Mr. Handley was the youngest son of the late Mr. T. Handley,who carried on business in Church-st. He re- ceived his elementary education at Mill-brow Academy, and afterwards laecame a student at the Liverpool Col- legiate, Shaw-M., conducted by the Rev Dean Howson. At a later date he entered the office of Messrs. John and Thomas Johnson, soap manufacturers, and thence went to the eat thlishment of Messrs. W. B. Gibson and Com- pany, nt which firm his brother James was a partner. After the death of Mr. Gibson, the style of the firm was . changed to that of James Handley and Company, the gentleman under notice.together with Mr. F. Beck, hav- ing becomf a member of the firm. Since the retirement of Mr. James Handley the concern has been conducted under the title of Handley and Beck. They have carried !on a large and successful business as iron merchants, ironmongers, fc., both in Runcorn and Widnes. rue deceased gentleman had been twice married, his first wife being Emily, third daughter of the late Mr. John Brookes, Runcorn, who was sister to the wife of Mr. Beck, his partner, by whom he had issue one son. His second wife was the second daughter of Mr. Joseph Littler, of Victoria-rnd, the issue being three sons and two daughters. _

-- With regard to public service, Mr. Handley was for several years a member of the Widnes Local Board, in connection with which he held the position of chairman of the Finance Committee. He continued his coanectiou , with this body until the incorporation of the town. He had been on the Runcorn School Board for eight years, and for a portion af that time presided over the proceedings of the School Attendance Committee. For a dozen years, he sat on the Burial Board, during which time he was elected to the chairmanship. It may be stated that the family had a continuous connection with the Burial Board throughout its existence. The father of the deceased was a member in 1858, before the cemetery was completed, and on his death Mr. James Handley was elected to fill the vacancy. This gentleman, after residing in Garston for some time, experienced the difficulties of efficiently and con- scientiously Wining his duties, and consequently re- tired when his brother, Mr. Thomas Handl-y, was selected to take his ,lace, and he continued his member- ship until the recent absorption of the Board by the Urban District Council. For four years he had a seat on the Runcorn Board of Guardians, being also a member of the Assessment Committee, and alter the death of the Rev. J. W. N. Tanner, he was appointed chairman ' of the House Committee. Mr. Handley had always displayed, since its inception, a keen interest in the Runcorn Musical Society,of which he was vice-president, and of which he had been treasurer. He was an active mi.mber of the society. Be was also a director of the Runcorn Gas Company. Politically, Mr. Handley was a sumach Liberal, and was ever ready to assist the cause to the utmost of his ability. Prior to the passing of tile Re distribution Act he was identified with the Mid-Cheshire Liberal Association, being hon. secretary for the Run- corn polling district. S nee then he has been a member ot the Executive Council of the Northwich Division. In religion he was a consistent Congregationalist, and his death will be severely, felt by those worshipping in Bethesda Church. Here for many years he had been a deacon,and had also been entrusted with other positions. Be was treasurer of the Sunday school. On July 2 id, 1894, he qualified as a magistrate for Cheshire, and hhie accession to the Bench enabled him to render valuable s•Tvices. General sympathy is extended to Mr.Handley's widow, six children, and to his aged mother, who has attained 82 years of age, and who has been ailing for a considerable time. The news of her son's death was broken to her gradually on Sunday afternsion, and it goes without saying that the information caused her poignant grief. MAGISTERIAL REFERENCE.

At Runcorn Petty H.-ssions, on Monday morning when the Bench was occupied by Messrs. Beery Reynolds (in the chair) and P. Speakman, allusion was mane to the death of Mr. Thomas Handley. The Chairman said: de- ' fore commencing the business of the day, it is with feel- ings of deep sorrow that I have to announce the death .of Mr. Thomas Handley. lam sure lam expressing the feelings of the whole of the members of the Bench when I say how sorry we are to lose so excellent a colleague, and when we think of the loss to his family as well as to the town in which he lived we are convinced that a valu- able life has been taken away from us. I feel sure that the whole Bench of magistrates, as well as the officers of the court, will unite with me in an expression of deep sympathy with his bereaved wife and family. This the Clerk will kindly forward.

WARRINGTON REVISION COURT.

The revision of the parliamentary and municinsl voters for the borough of Warrington was opened on Monday evecing by Mr. J. T. Foard, revising barrister, at the Warrington Town Hall. The Liberals were repre- sented by Mr. John Clayton, and the Conservatives by Mr. William Holbrook Mr. J. Lyan Whittle (town cicrk), Mr. S. Wilson (deputy town c lerk), Mr. Thomas Turistall (aqsistant overseer) and Mr. Garnett and Mr. F. Aukland (relieving officers) were also present.

FIRST CLAIM ALLOWED. Mr. Arthur James Snerwood claimed a lodger's vote for 44, Manchester-road. In reply to Mr. Clayton, the claimant said that he occupied two rooms—one down- stairs, and one uostairs. Mrs. Andrews was his land- lady. He paid 103. per week which included the use of furniture, gas, coal,etc. The rateable value was £22 gross. The claim was allowed.

NOT A FAMILY ARRANGEMENT. Mr. B. Faircloagh made a lodger's cl iirn for rooms at the house of his father, Mr. Richard Taylor Fairclough, Mersey Mills. Mr. Clayton : What rooms do you occupy ? I occupy a room on the ground floor, and one on the second fl ,or.—Do you have your meals separate from the family? Sometimes I do.—Do the rest of the family use the room ? They never do.—You can lock it up ? Yes.—How much do you pay? I pay 7.1. 61. per week.—What does that include ? Gas, and the cleaning of the rooms.—ln reply to his Honour, Mr. Tonstall said that the rateabls value of the house was £35. Further questioned, the claimant said that he was a mill manager apart from his fattier. Mr. Clayton said that it was very probable that this was a family arrangement,but hisElonour allowed the claim saying that there was al ways doubt in these cases,but this appeared to be bona-fide.

-AN ORGANIST OBTAINS A VOTE. Mr. W. Henry Payton, organist of Wycliffe Congregational Church, claimed a lodger vote for premises at 12, Bewsey-road.—The applicant informed Mr. Holbrook that he had two rooms set apart for his own use entirely, 10s. per week being the rent paid for them.—Mr. Clayton intimated to the Revising Barrister that no I" irliamentary vote was claimed for the house. There was a good margin of value.—The vote was given.

ANOTHER CLERICAL CLAIMANT.

The Rev. (1. B. Thurston claimed a lodger's vote for rooms at 20, Salisbury-st.—The applicant was repre- sented by the Rev. H. R. M. Hutt, who informed Mr. ' Clayton that a roam on the ground floor and also one on the second floor were occupied by the rev.gentlernan for whom he appeared. He (Mr. Hutt) did not, however, definitely kri,)w how much the Rev. G. B. Thurston paid for the hire of the rooms. He thought the items for board And lodging were separate, and amounted in the total to about 303.—Mr. J. L. Tuustall, replying to the R-vising Barrister, said that the value of the house was £3s.—Mr. Clayton : I am told that 163. per week cover the rev. gentleman's board and lodging.—The Rev.H. R. M. Hutt : I think that sum only represents lodging. But will inform Mr. Thurston, and he will appear himself, and will answer you more definitely.—The Revising Bar- rister expressed a wish for more information before he ' decided to allow the vote. He would adjourn this par- ticular claim until the appearance of the rev. gentleman. —At a later hour the Rev. G. B. Thurston arrived, and in answer to Mr. Clayton's cross-examination. said he paid 164. per week for rooms alone. Was It likely that he would able to get food and lodging in Salisbury- St. for 163. per week ? There were six rooms in the house, he thought.—Mr. Clayton submitted that there was not value enough to support the claim.-1 he Re- vising Barrister considered that there, was, and allowed the claim.

AN OATH DEMANDED„

The Conservatives claimed, on behalf of David Slater, a lodger's vote for 5, Williamson square.—Cross-ex- amined by Mr. Clayton, he said th4t before going to his present residence be lodged in Gilbert-street. That was about eight months ago. Being pressed, he denied that he bad gone from Gilbert-st. to Admiral-st. to live, before going to Williatnaon-aquare.—Mr. Clayton in- fol med the Revising Barrister that his instructions were that the applicant in this case had lodged with a person named Mulkearns, at 5, Admiral-st., before going to Williamson square. He should like,therefore,his Honour to put the applicant upon oath with regard to the mat- ter.—The Revising Barris*er assented, and after being sworn, Slater was asked if he did not go to live at 5, Admiral-,t., after leaving Gilbert-st. The oath had evi- dently its effect for the claimant then replied in the affirmative, and the claim was not allowed. TUESDAY'S PROCEEDINGS.

NOT CIUITE SUFFICIENT VALUE.

The Liberals applied on behalf of Francis Ed ward Norman, lot. a lodger vote for the rooms he occupied in Bewsey-road. His landlord appeared to support the claim, and said Mr. Norman occupied a bedroom and had the use of the sitting room. Mr. Holbrook elicited the fact that the house itself was only rated at £l4. gross and £l2 net. The landlord said he paid 7s. 63. a week rent. Fhe Revising Barrister said the claimant must have exclusive use of the qualifying rooms. As he had only the use of a sitting-room in this case, he thought it was just outside the margin.

A SUCCESSFUL OBJECTION.

The Liberals objected to Joseph Mirrott, who was on the list for 17. Foundry-st. Mrs. Marrott appeared,and in answer to Mr. Clayton she said she was the owner and occupier of the shop at 17, F ,undry-st. His Honour said if that was the case,the husband's name must come off the list. It was struck off accordingly.

LIBERAL CLAIMS. James Walsh, 6, Union-street, claimed an occupation vote. In reply to Mr. Holbrook he said his mother was the tenant but he paid the rent. He had two other brothers. One was an independent man and he was out in India at present. The other was a professional pugil- ist. He was the only one at home and was the support of his mother. The claim was allowed.

Patrick Riley claimed for a house at 7, Hughes'-court. Mr. Holbrook asked him where he had lived before going there. Riley tried to tell Mr. Holbrook, but ultimately gave up the attempt and exclaimed apparently in des- pair, " I can't speak hardly, lam not good English." The Revising Barrister suggested that the name was Pierpoint-st.,aiad the claimant said that was the name. The claimant, on being pressed by Mr. Holbrook, ultim- ately admitted that his brother-in-law was tenant on the election day,and the Revising Barrister promptly refused to allow the claim.

The Court adjourned at a quarter past eleven. WEDNESDAY'S PROCEEDINGS. _

Mr.Foard resumed the revision itf the lists on Wednes- day morning.

A LEGITIMATE OBJECTION. The Liberals objected to the name of Mr.J.F ulton Smith remaining on the list for 14. Museum-st., on the ground that he had left the house and now lived in North Wales.—ln answer to Mr. Clayton, Mr. Fulton Smith said his residence was now in North Wales. tie left Museum-st. during the fint week in July.—Mr. Clayton said that was his objection. Mr. Smith left Warrington before the expiration of the qualifying year.—Mr.Smith : But I am still responsible for the house ; I still pay the rant and shall continue to do so until next Christmas.— Mr. Clayton said they had already had a case similar to this, and it was decided that where a person left a house and did not intend to return he forfeited his vote.—Mr, Smith : But I do intend to return and reside again in this house. The Revising Barrister asked if he was to understand that Mr. Smith had left the house and had given up the tenancy, because if so,someone else might be qualified.— Mr. Smith said he was still the tenant and no one else was qualified. Fie had, in fact, certain furniture in the house still. He had removed from the house simply as an experiment to see how the change would affect his children's health.—The Revising Barrister thought that the objection, under the circumstances, had not been made out, and he should allow Mr. Smith to retain his vote.—Mr. Smith asked if he was entitled to expenses, but the Revising Barrister said he thought he was not, as the objection seemed a legitimate one. A COUNCILLOR'S TOTE OBJECTED 'co

- The Liberals objected to the vote of Councillor W. P. Skelton ; who was on the list for a stable at 6, Cloth Hall Yard.—Mr. Clayton asked Councillor Skelton if he was the managing director for W. P. and W. MISLEADING.

The Conservatives objected to the name of a woman remaining on the register. It appeared that the woman's name was Marv, but Mr. Holbrook had found out that the name on the rent book was Maria, and he accord- ingly objected to anybody named Mary having a vote for the house in question. Mary now appeared and proved that she was the Maria named on the rent book, and the objection fell through. Mary claimed expenses and the Revlsing Barrister allowed her 2s. Mr. Holbrook protested against having to par expenses in such a case, considering that the name on the rent book was Maria and not Mars, hut the Revising Barrister pointed out that the objection was that the woman was not the occu- pier whereas she was. She was not given an opportun- ity of making a declaration that her name was Mary and not Maria.

===== 0002601_18950914_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
A CONFLICT OF DATES.

John Clare, 17, Howard-street, Little Sankey, claimed a vote. Mrs. Clare appeared to support the claim. Mr. Clayton objected to its being allowed, stating that Mr. Clare only came from Penketh to Howard-street, in October last year. Mrs. Clare said they came on the 9th July. Asked if she would swear that that was the date, she said she would, and on being sworn she repeated that the date was the 9th July, and the vote was allowed. Mr. Clayton said his information was that the date was in October.

FOOTBALL.

THE NORTHERN RUGBY UNION.

Another meeting of the Emergency Committee of the Northern Union was held at the George Hotel, Hudders- field, on Thursday night. There were present Mr. J. Nichols, Halifax, in the chair, and Messrs. J. Clifford, Huddersfield ; H. Sewell,Leeds ; J.Warren, Warrington; and J. Platt, Oldham (hon. secretary). An applica- tion was made by Samuel Hall, Swinton, a player who had been suspended by the English Rugby Union for permission to play with the clubs in the Union, Union. The application was refused. It appeared that when Hall left Swinton some time ago to go to America he was presented with a sum of £25 by the Swinton club, and he was then suspended by the English Rugby Union, and has not yet been rein- stated. After referees had been appointed for matches on the 21st inst., the fixtures were then completed, and the meeting proceeded to consider the by-laws and rules governing the competition. The business had not con- cluded when the C)mmittee adjourned till Tuesday next at the Spread E.gle Hotel, Manchester, at five o'clock. Among the subjects considered was a suggestion to re- duce the number of forwards by two, to dispense en- tirely with line outs on the ground that they invariably resulted in scrimmages, and to play with a round ball instead of an oval ball, as it is believed there would be more accuracy in dribbling, passing, &c. The following are the local fixtures for the remainder of the season : January 4.—Runcorn v. Oldham, Warrington v. Wid- nes, Brighouse Rangers v. St. Helens. January 11.—Liversedge v. STATUS OF NORTHERN UNION PLANERS.

IMPORTANT ACTION BY THE NORTHERN COUN- TIES ATEILETIC ASSOCI&TION. There was a meeting of the Executive of this Associa- tion in Manchester on Weinesday night which was of a more important character than usual in the face of the uncertainty existing among athletes generally as to how the status of those runners would be affected, whi were members of the clubs in the Northern Bub

- Union. Mr. T. M. Abraham, of Crewe, a meinioer of • the Executive of the Association and a representative to , the Amateur Atheletic Association, had given notice of his intention toinquire if a ptaer who plays under the I rules of the N!rthern Poothali Union, and receivespay- meat for broken time, forfeits his am iteur ' status. In addition he had a proposal on the agenda to the effect that the secretary of the Amateur Athletic Association he requested to convene a meeting of the General Committee as early as convenient after the 19!:h inst. to coniider what attitude shall be taken with regard to Rugby football players Wiko receive payments for broken time. The meeting was presided over by Mr. R. Newton thodes, Bradfords Previous to the business being entered up in a long argu- ment ensued as to whether the deliherat!ons should be thrown open to the Press or conducted in private. Several of the members of the committee were in favour of the admission of the reporters, but it is understood that the Yorkshire representatives and others demurred to this, and it was ultimately decided to furnish an official note at the conclusion. Tie discussion was interesting, and after Mr. Abraham had withdrawn the first item standing in his name, the mai-wits, of the com- mittee spoke on his resolution to to, effect that the secretary of the Amateur Athle.tic Association be requested to call a meeting of the General Committee. Messrs. Beardsall, Hirst, Rhodes, B .nnett. Abraham, and others all expressed their views, which were naturally of a conflicting character, the majority - stoutly contending that payment f -at broken time should not rob a man of his amateur status, whilst Mr. Abra- hams, in particular, raised an opposite contention. The end of the discursive discussion was that Mr. Abraham's proposition was slightly amended and then passed,beiag made to read as follows :—" That tha secretary of the Amateur Athletic Association be ref/ iested to convene a meeting of ale General Committee as early as conveni- ent in November to consider what attitude shall be taken with reg.rd to Rugby footbal players who re- ceive paymmt for broken time." In a hlition to this, the following further resolution of great importance was passed :—" In the opinion of this committee a man who does siot receive payment for !srok-n time can way un- der the Northern Rugby Union rules without forfeiting his amateur status, but whether a placer who does re- ceive payment for broken time is a professional must be settled et a meeting of the Amateur Athletic Associa- tion proposed to be held in November."

THE MORECAMBE DISASTER.

THE INC/DES-F.—REMARKABLE EVIDENCE. Some remarkable evidence was given at the adjourned inquiry into the death of the three victims of the More- cambe disaster. The manager of the Morecambe Pier Company said that in February last the Board received a report from Mr. Worthington, civil engineer, suggest- ing a practical reconstruction of the pier. He then bad his doubts, expressed in a minute, ahout the safety of the pier. The report was not acted upon by the Board. Mr. Worthington said that after inspecting the pier he recommended certain alterations and improvements, the , cost of which would be over £5OOO. He prepared esti- mates and plans for the directors in February last, but be had not since been consulted about the work. The cause of the disaster was corroded bolts. The oscillat.,. of the pier loosened the bolts and caused a continued corrosion. One defective bolt had been reduced from a weight of about 41b. to liib. The witness assented to a remark of the Coroner that the pierhead was "worn out, rotten, and insecure." There were, he said, hundreds of empty boltholea. When he made his report he did not expect a disaster, hut he certainly contemplated that something would at once he done to stengthen the pier. The inquest was adjourned until the 25th inst. In the meantime a Board of Trade Inspector is to examine thc pier.

ItIJNCO liN.

There are now good reasons for believing that the Aluminium Company, London and Birmingham, have secured land for the building of large chemical works in the immediate neigh bourhood of Runcorn, It is also reported that the Salt Union are proposing to make a new departure, and t hat they have par- chased land for the purpose of etne-ructing chemi- cal works, now or in the near future, close to where they at present carry oil operations in manufactur- ing salt, at Weston. The establishment of these works will, it need scarcely be said, give an im- petus to the trade of Runcorn which has for soma time now been in a depreseed condition.

PROGRESS OF ELEIIENTA.RY

EDUCATION.

ACCOMMODATION, ATTENDANCES, AND GRANTS

IN WARRING WN SCHOOLS.

The annual report of the Committee of the Privy Council on Education j.,=s: issued for 1895 shlws that there were last years 5,198,741 children on the registers of the elementary schools ~1 Eegland and Wales, as cm- pared with 5,128,373 in 1893 and 5,008,979 1892. The proportionate growth of the number of children in the schools is greater than the estimated growth ot the popu- lation of the country. There has also been a great im- provement in the regularity of attendance. The number of frce schsiols and free scholars shows a steady increase, and lant year 18,289 schools were frae as compared with 15,914 ire the previous year. Of the 5,189.741 scholars only 821,000 pay fees, the large num- ber of 4.277,741 receiving their education free. informed that the committee were for several hours oc- cupied in arranging fix ' tures and had also before th!m an imeortant question in the shape of an application from Joe Smith, of Salford, to play with the Union. Smith, it will be remembered, was suspended for pro- fessionalism la t season, unaer the rules of the Rugby Union. and as one of the reasoes why the clubs which form the Northern Ullion seceded frern the Rugby Unien was the refusal of the former organisation to allow payment to players for brokeii ttnae, unusual interest attached to the decision in Smith's case. After a long consideration,the committee refused the application,and thus made it manifest that they distinctly disfavour pro- fessionalism, apart from the question of broken time. Another matter which was oiscussed was the constitu- tion of the Committee of Mamie ,rneut, but nothing was decided upon this point, though it was suegested that the body should eorfsist of fifteen representatives-- , namely, five from Laneaehire clubs, five from Yorkshire clubs, and five from clubs from other counties which may in the future join the Union,the idea being to widen the area of the organisation. Attention was also given to the rules which are to govern the Ueion, and they are to be Again considered at future meetings of the Commit- tee. NORTHERN RUUBY LEAGUR

WARRINGTON v. HUNSLET.

The Warrington club having joined the Northern Rugby Union,they commenced their season's programme by meeting the well-known Yorkshire club, Elunslet, at the Wilderspool enclosure on Saturday. A large amount of interest has been taken in Warrington in the recent developments in Rugby foothall,and general satisfaction has been expressed with the action of the local club in throwing in their lot with the rest of the secessionists, one reason being that such a first-class list of fixtures has been arranged. The day on Saturday was a little too sultry for careering about a field for 70 minutes, but nevertheless, this feat was done, and the players gave an excellent exposition of Rugby football. About 6,000 spectators assembled to witness the two clubs finally commit themselves to the new order of things by playing in the "close "season. Mr. Slevin, of Wigan, acted as referee,and a little after the advertised time, the follow- ing teams took the field to fight for their first points in the Northern League :—Warrington Boscow,full back; Barber, O'Brien, Burton, and Carey, three-quarters ; Bate and Foclen, half backs ; Turner, Taylor, DAM, Nevins, Berry, Sankey, Saunders, and Swift, forwards. Hunslet : W. Goldthorpe, full back ; Hannah, Wright, Townsend, and Mitchell, three-quarters ; Lapping aid Gillings, half backs ; Kaye, Walsh, Deacon, fdawson, Rubry, Barraclough, Greenwood and Hill, forwards. Barber won the toss and set the Tykes to defend the railway goal, and during the first half Hunslet were in- coneenienced by the sun. Kaye kicked off, and the re- turn by O'Brien found the centre. Here play remedied for a time, but gradually Hunslet worked their way to ri.e 25 flag. They did not become really dangerous, however, for Boscow,Barber, and Burton were too alert. A round of passing between Burton, Berry, Taylor, and Swift soon restored lost ground. A number of line mite here took place,and Turner was especially prominent by the manner in which he took possession of the ball. Bate now broke away, and was not floored until he reached Goldthorpe at the visitors' quarter flag. The game pro- ceeded with alternate advantage and a fine hit of play by the Hunslet men was loudly applauded. Walsh ob- taining possession,raced away, and being well supported ' the ball quickly pissed into the hands of Gillings,Towns- end, and Mitchell, the last Vass however being forward. This put Hunslet on th.: off ensivi , and Warringten had some anxious me ments,but Boscow was not found want- ing. Hunslet, to use a cricketim; phrase,seemed to have become set in the old lines, when suddenly, Bate smartly gathering up the ball after a scrimmage passed to Barber. The skipper passed off and after a good run passed to Burton. When near the 25 flag he passed to O'Brien, and that player was pulled to earth within a yard or two of the coveted line. This was a narrow escape indeed for Hunslet. The latter carried the ensu- ing scrimmage, and e gain took play to the opposite end. IA bit of play now ensued which fairly electrified the 1 spectators. From a 'scrimmage near the Warrington goal posts, Foden go possession, and passed to Bate. The latter passed to Burton, and thence the ball passed through the hands of the majority of the team, the for- wards passing as well as the backs,and before Foden was floored finally,the ball had got to the opposite end of the field. This effort truly deserved a score. A free kick against Warrington for an informality spoiled the ad- vantage gained, but this was well counterbalanced by a single handed or rather single footed dribble by oankey. This was supplemented by Burton, and had not the referee been unintentionally in the way, a certain try would have been scored by Turner or Taylor. The ball however struck the referee when d very short distance from the line, and thus gave the Hunslet men time to stave off the danger by kicking dead. The remainder of the half was pretty even, but Barber had very hard lines in missing a score on one occasion. The interval arrived with a clear sheet. The play in the second half was very attractive, each side nutting in some pretty passing. Turner commenced operations, and there being no return, play settled in the Yorkshire territory, and continued there some time, but Goldthorne was exceptionally safe, and Warrington could not score, although they were very near it some- times. The Hunslet team gradually recovered lost ground, and along kick by Mitchell, reached Boscow on his own goal line. His return was charged down by Walsh within a few yards off the line,to the great dismay of Warrington. Fortunately, however, it rolled into touch, and from the line out, Warrington gained a little ground, through the instrumentality of Turner and Tay- lor. This relief was but brief, and the attack was again renewed. Boscow attength made his mark, but Walter Goldthorpe getting possession after :the kick, he rushed away to the open, and from near half way, he dropped a splendid goal, and this drew first blood, putting his side from points ahead. The Hunslet team showed their appreciation of this performance, but their joy was of short duration. Warrington was fairly on the mettle now, and after Turner's kick off, thoy played with as lunch vigour as th,m4h the game had only just c mene,ed, and fairly outplayet their oppanents. A des- perate rush was then made, and the ball was got over the line. Fader' dropping on the ball he opened the scor- ing account for Warrington for the present season. The game was not yet won,for Warrington were still a point behind, and as the try was scored far out near the touch line, there were many anxious glances at Burton as he got the ball in position from the place kick. H. took an unusual time from this, but the result justified him, and terrific cheering ensued when from the difficult angle, the ball was seen to sail over the cross bar, and Warrington were a point ahead. The game continued with unabated vigour, but it was chiefly in mid flelii,and when the whistle blew for the cessation of hostalities, Warrington had won a well earned victory, by a placed goal (5 points), to a dropped goal (4 points), and thus gained their first two points in the League.

ASSOCIATION.

WARRINGTON v. RONCORN.—The initial fixture OR the Warrington Card was played on Saturday last at Run- corn. There was a good number of spectators. It was noticed that E. Connor, J. Mather and Evans , were not in the team. Williamson also was a notable absentee. Norman kicked off, and putting his left wing in posses- sion at the right moment enabled Connor and Selby to make good headway, the latter's shot going wide, from the kick out the honaester's forwards made tracks hut feund a stumbling block in Limmond who, kicking judi- ciously placed his side again on the aggressive, Jewkes's shot being finely cleared. Warrington still kept up the pressure and experienced hard lines on several occasions. Half time arriving with no score. On resuming Runcorn made play on the left but Marshall, being on the spot, broke up the combination finely and with a huge kick quickly transferred play to the other end. Not to be denied, however, Runcorn again came down and eluding the hacks Walker drew first blood with a daisy cutter, I Webster having no chance to save. At the restart War- rington came away with a rush. Smithson transferring o Conner at close quarters. his shot being cleared at the expense of a cortr‘r ; the ball was now hobbing up and down in front of goal for some moments, until lewkes shot over the bar. Through some good back play War- rington were quickly in front of goal once more, Smith- son again passing across to Connor, that player making a good attempt which rebounded off the Runcorn goal- keeper in the vicinhy of Selby, who promptly notched the equalising' point. This was the only other point scored, the whistle blowing with the score one goal each. . .

_ ST. ELPHIN'S V. CHESTER RESERVE.—This match SCRS played on the ground of the latter on Saturday before about 500 spectators. Dugdale kicked off for the visitors, who criickly made tracks for the home goal and, from a pass by Dandy, Hickenbottom opened their account with a grand shot against which the goalkeeper had no chance. From the re-start the home right wing took the ball well down and a miskick by Hunter gave them an opening which Lipsham took advaetage of, equalizing the score but the Saints were not to be denied and soon made the score into two, by the aid of Dendv but about a minute after Lipsham again made the score equal. From the kick off the visitors fairly kept their opponents on the defence King adding two more goals with long shots which completely beat the home custodian, half time arriving with the score 4.2 in favour of the visitors. Shortly before time Hatton made the score into 5 which left St.Elphins with a well earned victory by 5 goals to 2. HOLY TRINITY V. BRICK-STREET MISSION.—rhe first matt:ill of the season between these two teams took place at Howley on Saturday. The Trinitarians won the toss, and commenced hostilities with the wind. J. Pugh opened the scoring, with a high shot. The Trinitarians warmed to their work, Appleton passed to Haddockand he centred to J. Pugh, who scored the second goal. Nothing more was scored up to half time, when the homesrers were leading by two goals to nil. The second half was evenly contested, and exchange kicking by the resp,ctivo backs was the order, and an appeal for hands by the Mission in front of the Trinity goal came to naught. The visitors played with more determination, and Legge scored with a good shot, which Taylor failed to fist out. Another rush by the Trinity kept the Mis• sion defence busy, and a fine centre by Rigby into he goal mouth resulted in %V. Miller scoring. Nothing more was scored, and when the whistle sounded the Trinitari- ans ran out winners of a good game by three goals to one.

RECOG I'l TON ME 111 TING AT WARRINGTON.

THE NEW WESLEYAN MINISTER.

A meeting was held at the Bold-street Wesleyan school- room on Monday evening for the purpose of welcoming the Rev. W. G. I riggs, who has come to labour in War- rington Wesleyan circuit for the next three years. There have been several changes iR the circuit of late. The Rev. John Kirtlan, who has held the office of superinten- dent of the circuit for the past three years, has left and gone to the Doncaster circuit. The Rev. Owen Watkins who was formerly the second minister, has now been app Anted superintendent, and the former third minister The Rev. G. D. Mason has been promoted to the second. The vacancy for the third minister has been filled by the Rev. W. G. Triggs, for the purpose of welcoming whom, the meeting was held on Monday evening. There was a geed attendance, and the chair was occupied by one of the circuit stewards, Mr. R. G. Henshall. There were also present en the platform, the Revs. Owen Watkins, G. D. Mason, W. G. Triggs, ald John Gunnell, Councillor Shaw Green, and Messrs. R. Garnett J. P.,W. C. Nicholson and E. %own. On the trent of the platform was the word "Welcome" in large letters, and the room was also bedecked with flags. The CHAIRMAN in his opeting speech,after apologising for the absence of Mr. William Richardson, the senlor circuit steward, said that they were met that night to welcome their newly appointed minister and his wile,the Rev. W. G. and Mrs. Triggs. He was afraid that com- bog from the sunny south to the town of Warrington with its smoke and smells, must have been rather a rude shoJk. They could not give him the beautiful landscapes of the south, but they could prove that the Methodists of Warrington were as loyal to their ministers and as earnest, warm and hearty as their brethren in the south (Applause.) Personally, he was very pleased to offer Mr. Triggs a welcome that night, as to him and to Mr. Bennett it represented the successful issue of a tough struggle which was fought over the body of Mr. Triggs at the Conference at Plymouth. (Laughter.) They had been informed by the Stationing Committee at Plymouth that Mr. Triggs was to go to the Manchester mission and that for Warrington, the name of Mr. Daniel Eva was to be put down. Ile (Mr. Henshall), and Mr. Richardson saw Mr. Watkins on Monday morning, and that gentle- man told them that if they wished to get Mr. Triggs, they must fight like Kilkenny cats. (Laughter.) They did fight, and now he was glad to say that they had got Mr. Triggs. (Applause.) They had also that night a new superintendent, and a new second minister in the Rev. Owen Watkins, and the Rev. G. D. Mason. He hoped that the three ministers weuld have a prosperous year, and he trusted that Mr. and Mrs. Triggs would , s)on settle down and that they Would be a blessing to the circuit. (Applause.) The Chairman added that letters of apology for non-attendance had been received from Miss Resale Dutton,Mr. Brough,Dr. T. S. Smith J.P.,and Mr. J. W. Hayward J.P. Mr. W. C. NicnoesoN, said that he, as representing the Bold-street society, offered to their newly appointed minister a very cordial welcome to the Warrington cir- cuit, and to Bold-street. He (the sp)aker) was in a very unique position as a local preacher. He was a local preacher of the fourth generation. (Applause. His great grandfather was appointed to take charge of a church society and to conduct monthly services near Bedford and was a preacher for many years. His grandfather - was a local preacher in the same circuit, and his father occupied that honoured position for many years. Ho (Mr. Nicholson) also received his credentials as a fully accredited local preacher in the same circuit. He well remembered coming to Warrington, r and his name being at the bottom of the plan, and he was now number four with 31 good men and true below him. He offered a loyal welcome to Mr. Triggs on behalf of the local preachers, and he trusted that a most successful year would be spent. Mr. R. GARNETT said that that meeting augured well for the future of the circuit. He was glad to 808 the heartiness and manifestation they had of it at that meet- ing. It augured well for future progressive work. Be thought that the Christianity of the present day was in many respects very mechanical. The services were mechanical, the Sabbath observances were mechanical, and there was a want of that earnest spirit which he seemed to remember in his younger days. They used to sing the hymns for believers fighting such as " Sur- rounded by a host of foes,stormed by a host of foes with- in." Now they had the singing done by a cultured choir who eang " Art thou weary, art thou languid, art thou sore distresed, Come to me saith one and coining, ISe at rest." (Laughter.) There was too much ease in Zion, and too little earnestnees. He prayed that the blessing

of God would rest upon the man who had been ap- pointed the minister for the coming year. He had heard it stated that God never removed one servant without sending a better one. That might have been true or un- true, but he really believed that in Mr. Triggs they had got a man who would serve them well, whose heart was in his work, and be hoped and trusted that by the end of a year's work there would be the ingathering of many souls. (Applause.) The Rev. W. G. Tams, who was very warmly re- ceived, said that he had heard on competent authority that Warrington was absolutely destitute of enthusiasm, but he would have to nail that to the counter as a lie after that night. He felt uncomfortable—they would all understand that—(laughter)—and he hardly knew what to say, but he thanked them most heartily for the most cordial welcome they had given to Mrs. Triggs and himself. They were welcoming the new minister and his wife. He hoped they would learn to appreciate the man and his wife. (Hear, hear, and applause.) He had no love for recognition meetings. He was that night on exhibition. (Laughter.) Het hoped they were satis- fied. (Renewed laughter.) It was not pleasant to be exhibited, hut he hoped that he might exhibit himself in the homes of the people, and get to know them,and nave as hearty a welcome there—(applause)—as they had given him that night. It was his most earnest desire to get inside their homes as quickly as possible. He had asked for a visiting list, but until he had that he was helpless. Of Warrington he had heard a great many pleasant things,he had heard nothing but good,save that scandal he had already reported to them. Everybody told him that it would do him good to go from the luxurious south to the north. He trusted that by coming north, he would be strengthened spiritually. He had been told that the south loved body while the north loved brains, that the north went in for force, and the south for finish, the north for sense, and the south for sentiment. (Laughter.) He had also been told that the Lancashire folk cared a great deal more for the substance of a man's sermon, while in the south, style went for everything. He did not know whether that was correct ; anyhow, Mr. Nicholson said that the Methodists adapted themselves to the circum- stances and conditions of the moment,and he hoped that they would do so with him. He trusted that the services ho held would be hearty, and he noticed that at his first Sonday in Bold-street, he did not bear any " Amens." He did not know whether they had been turned out. He would like a judicious sprinkling of them, although he did not care tor a continual outburst. He was glad that lie had two such colleagues as Mr. Watkins and Mr. Mason, and he was sure that he would be able to work well with them. With regard to the future he wished to say a little. He was atraid that the friends would not always agree with him. He did not know whether he would al ways express himself in scholastic or cultured English. Be might sometimes offend their taste, and Speak too plainly. He liked to be understood, and when he stood up to preach he did not want anybody to go away with mistaken ideas as to what he did say. He would not go out of his way to offend anybody, and he invited criticism. If anybody had anything to say worth listening to, tie was prepared to listen, but be did not want captious criticism. (Hear, hear.) He would also be very glad to go into the Sunday school if he was wanted there. He was not going to enter the Sunday school if he was not wanted there, but if they asked him to come, he was at their service. (Applause.) They must, however, not expect him to give five minutes in the morning service to the children. He could not do it. He could not possibly preach for five minutes to the chil- dren. He would give an address whenever they liked in the Sunday school, in the afternoon, but no five minutes, if they pleased,in the morning. He would make himself ridiculous. He believed in consolidation. He had no desire to connect his name with any novel departure in that circuit, and he had no ambition to be an innova- tor. He did not feel it to be his ambition to make experiments, but he would take his share in the work which was being carried on. He would see what their welcome was by the way they attended the week evening services. He hoped to see the attendances go up, and unless he saw that,he would not be satisfied. He thanked them very much for their welcome and hoped they would get along with one another and that they woula learn to like and to love one another. (Applause.) Mr. EDWIN BROWN welcomed Mn, Triggs on behalf of the Wesleyans et Lymm. He went on to speak of the pleasure, and of the honour he felt in being the senior local preacher on the plan. He hoped that the Warring- ton circuit would stand side by side and shoulder to shoulder with the ministers, and that the coming year would be ore of prosperity. Councillo, SHAW GREEN then spoke on behalf of the Bewsey-roadrhurch,and the trustees. He felt sure that the choice they had made was a wise one, and that Mr. Triggs would prove a blessing to the circuit. He asked Mr. Triggs to come to Silver-street school, and see the work which was being carried on there. They were do- ing a good work there,although it was in a rather rough neighbourhood. Mr. Green then proceeded humour- ously to defend Warrington against the charges that its surroundings were not beautiful, and expressed his opinion that Mr. Triggs would be able to find some Splendid country within a very narrow radius of War- rington. He concluded by wishing Mr. Triggs a very pleasant and prosperous time in Warrington. The Rev. OWEN WATKINS proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman for presiding over the meeting. He Said that be rejoiced in the coming of Mr. Triggs which he was sure would be a great blessing to the circuit. He hoped that the congregat ions would support him and the other ministers with their prayers. He felt sure that they hail a capital superintendent—(laughter)—and the , superintcndent had two good colleagues. It would not be their fault if the work did not prosper. He was pleased to join in the welcome to Mr. Triggs and his wife and he trusted that they would have a good time in Warrington.

Aker, •

The Rev. G. D. Mums) seconded the vote of thanks. He said that there was a great tendency to leave every- thing to the minister, not simply in Warrington but throughout the Church of Jesus Christ. He wished the church would realise their responsibilities, and if they did, he had no doubt what the result would be. He ex- tended a hearty welcome to Mr. and Mrs. Triggs who were his neighbours. He trusted that they would else be colleagues and brethren who would help each other in the work. (Applause.) The motion was carried unanimously, and the Chair- man briefly replied. During the meeting, the choir sang the anthem, "Praise the Lord," and the glee, " Let the hills re- sound." Miss Jeans also sang "The better land," and Miss Collin, " Children Asleep." Refreshments were also handed round during the interval. The meetink. was brought to a close with the pronouncing of the benedic- tion.

SOUTH AFRICAN GOLD MINES.

The Hammond' Matabele Gold Mines Development Company. This is the name of a venture which has acquired 150 mining claims in the Lower Gwelo District. The claims were selected and reported on by Mr. Andrew Robert Hammond who will act as managing director for the Company. There are fort, claims on the Rose Reef, from which assays and pannings have given from 4ozs. to 7ozs. of gold FT ton; and the other claims are on the Richard, Arctic, lngon-Yama, and Loublie Reefs, all of which show former workings, evidently of a profitable character. The capital of the Company is £200,000 in £1 shares, of which 130,000 have been issued, and 17s. paid. There thus remain 70,000 to be issued whenever further working capital is required. Active dealings have taken place in these shares on the London Stock Exchange at the price ot 9s. 6d.

THE CA.THOLIC TRUTH SOCIETY.

The business of the annual Conference of the Catholic Truth society commenced at Bristol on Tuesday. In the morning the ateution of the meeting was devoted to the consideration of a series of papers on the best means of carrying out the directions contained in the Porr's recent letter by means of lectures, tracts, and the press. Subsequently, papers cn the position of the Catholic schools in this country and elementary and secondary educatir'n, were read. In the course of the discussion in the morning the Rev. J. H. Boucher, of Clutton,a clergyman of the Church of England,addressed the meeting, an,d said the letter from the Pope to the English people could not but have touched the hearts of everyone,whether Roman, Anglican, or Protestant. Be took the opportunity as an Anglican clergyman,to protest strongly against the bitterness often used by a great many of his brethren against Roman Catholics. A tele- gram was subsequently sent to the Pope presenting the homage of these present and asking for the Apostolic blessing.

—The man who attunpted to explode a bomb at Messrs. Rothschild's batik in Paris last week has been identified. The police say he is an Anarchist, but the motive of the crime alleged against him appears to be the personal one that he felt aggrieved at having been dismissed from the workshops of the Northern Railway Company.

THE POLITICAL CYCLONE.

BY Mn. H. WHITHAN.

Now for a season of rest. Judging from the English papers I have received since the election. I conclude that the Tory party has landed itself, not high and dry and out of danger, but in about the worst dilemma it has been in during this century. To Salisbury and his supporters of the Colonel North stamp, it must be a great relief to feel that the insatiate maw of the Radical party is closed for a while. What if a Radical programme has been promised the people by his right honourable friend J. Chamberlain. the new Premier need be under no obligation to any one now,for the free and indepen- dent voter has gone sturdily up to the ballot box and shown by his vote that he is tired of so much , legislation and wants a season of rest. In this laudable desire the wealthy, who know more about the death dues than the one who creates the wealth does, will heartily sympathise. It was getting time to put down the Socialist nonsense and the I claims of the I.L.P. If these envious upstarts were not satisfied with having a good dozen labour re- pres.,ntatives in Parliament, why, it only required lan effort to bundle the whole lot into the streets. Horn ,t John Burns alone keeps his seat, and even he ga, e such satisfaction to his London friends that ciey reduced his majority from 1,559 to 244 votes. The " United we stand and divided we fall "party has improved matters to the extent of making one take the place of the five labour mem- bers London could boast of a few weeks ago. When [look over the voting list of the kingdom lam simply struck with awe at the wonderful amount of political sagacity and forethought dis- played by the advanced thinkers of the Radical party. In London the I.L.P. polled for their pet candidate in Fulham 196 out of 12,716 electors. In Bradford our old friend Ben Tillet rushed to the rear with 2,264 out of 11,200. Well, perhaps after all it was a "moral victory," and we have a possible six or seven years during which to find the " moral." It was too bad of West Ham to send Keir Hardie to the right about. He may find some solace now in admiring his red sash during the in- tervals left to him when chuckling over the success of the advice he gave his friends. Wherever the Lk?. or a Socialist opposed the Liberal candidate, success beamed on the Tory. Northing but what anyone with a grain of political sense might have foreseen. It has always been the same in America. Political parties gain their strength by steady growth and, as the seething element of reform works under,aud, bubbling up to the surface,create rings that gradually yet surely widen out until they embrace a majority that can do good and per- manent reform work. _ _ .

Well, the Tories are in, and with a big majority, and it is no waste of time to look around for the cause. We can venture to leave the parties for a while who believe that to split and divide is the road to success. They have thought that by break- ing away from the Liberal party, by putting candidates, and by withholding their votes they were ensuring their own success. Time alone will ' prove if this is so. ' I have, more than once, given my reasons why I think a steady support should be given the Liberal party, and, not to cry over spilled milk, a little dis- cussion over some of the points would not be amiss, advancing one or two further views. The history of the Liberal party in England is,broadly speaking, the history of the rise and progress of English commerce. True, we may in a few instances see families, like the Birleys of Manchester, connected with the factory, but the seats in the House of Commons prove that, as a rule, the mill and the field are respectively represented by the Liberal and the Tory. The Corn Laws were repealed by the mill owners, the factory acts were enacted by the owners of farms. It has been a case of tit for tat all the way down, each interest trying to spite the other, or, while the rogues fell out, the honest workman came to his own. To the man of noble birth and cultured tradition, buying in the cheapest market and selling in the dearest was an abomina- tion not to be thought of. In the same way the man who had worked at the loom, who as a self- made man became the owner of the loom, innoc- ently looked over the heals of the poor wretches toiling in his mills and longed for an opportunity to throw three acres and a cow at the head of the " free and independent slave of the squire." When Land Duty spoke of "taking a leap in the ' dark," and the embryomic Tory, H. Lowe, advised "teaching our masters," they but voiced a feeling that was in the breasts of all rich men ; nay, it was In the minds of all thoughtful men—that was,that with household suffrage pewer in the State would centre itself in the hands of the Masses, and would be used for the spoliation of the rich. Time has proved that all these gloomy forebodings were mere figments of the brain and not worth harbouring. The man who lives under the most degrading con- ditions, yet clings to sweet life. So the most poverty-stricken among us are often found the moat conservative of something some one else owns. The soldier will fight to the death in defence of a country in which a narrow plot of land only may be his useless own some day.

f should not like to have about me the least par- ticle of ingratitude, neither to individual, nor yet to party. Long years ago I had the picture of John Bright hanging on my walls, but the study of political and commercial history compelled me to consign that picture to the fire. I had listened to the People's Tribune, and I had read his speeches, and I am convinced that from his point of view he had the people's welfare at heart, but he was too strong a supporter of the Manchester school for me to admire. He was incapable of seeing the true inwardness of the cause of all the poverty he so much deplored. Yet I should have voted for him as a Liberal party man, feeling that I was 3limbing the political stairs surely and safely, one step at a time.

In the hands of the Tory party the glory and the prestige of the British nation have been placed so far as talk went. In the hands of the Liberal party the commerce of the nation has been safe. The cards have been roughly shuffled up, and the hand now held up is a strange one. The author of three acres and a cow,and the gentleman who some years ago Punch depicted as a roaring republican lion with the Prince of Wales at his feet, are now shining lights in a government pledged to do,— what ? How has this government come into exis- tence? One fact I may note and say no more about it—the Bung aud the Church. Yet, I must say a little about the church. Most people have ceased to give to it, as an Establishment, any credit for being, even remotely, connected with the teachings of Christ. It is un-Christ-like in all its teachings, its surroundings and its aspirations. Like the Church of Rome it seeks to be impressive,but it will not carry the Cross. Like its sister of Rome it seeks all the pomp and vanity of this world regardless of the cost in human blood; and a factor not to be made light of is the opposition of the two churches to the educational system inaugurated by W. H. Forster. Neither church is libera: in any sense of the word, nor can they ever be, in spite of all they Day say in their church magazines, and to deputa- tions of Liberal workingmen. I have before me copies of letters sent by the Princes of the Romish Church to the members of their churches. All and everything is to be put on one side, so that the

FIFTY PERSONS IMMERSED.

Shortly before eleven o'clock on Menday morning a lamentable accident occurred at the Morecambe pleasure pier through the collapse of a portion of the iron land- ing-stage for steam-boat traffic at the end of the pier. The large steamer Empress was drawing up at the pier head preparatory to making a pleasure trip to Black- pool, and the steps and gangways under the pier were crowded with would-be passengers, who had taken their tickets, and were waiting until the steamer was safely moored. On this being done, and communication effected between the stage and the steamer, there was a general rush for the latter, with the result that a portion of the stage, composed of iron gratings, and covering an area of about 15 square feet, gave way under the pres- sure, and a mass of struggling men, women, and chil- dren, numbering probably over 50, were instantly pre- cipitated into the water below before the eyes of hundreds of horrified spectators on the pier. Great excitement prevailed, in the midst of which life buoys and lines were promptly thrown from the steamer, the depth of the water toeing variously estimated at from five to eight feet. The officials connected with the steamers and pier dived in the water or clambered down the piles to the assistance of the submerged persons, whilst on the alarm being raised, fishermen and other volunteers rushed down the foreshore for a similar pur- pose. Those of the unfortunate persons who werecling- Mg to the pier or wreckages were quickly recovered and assisted to a place of safety. The majority of those, however, who were in the shallower water, and might ' have safely waded to the shore, in the confusion rustled towards the steamer, thus rendering their rescue more difficult, the tide rapidly flowing in at the time, and the water momentarily becoming deeper. The tide receding about seven o'clock in the evening a large body of police, fishermen,and other residents made a diligent search of the channel and sands about the scene of the accident, it being feared tbat other bodies might be entangled among the wreckage. This supposi- tion,unfortunately proved correct. After various articles ~f clothing, umbra las, &c., had been found, James Gardner, fisherman,pulled the body of a young man out of the water. Shortly after,Captain Phompsnn recovered the body of a female which was afterwards identified by Mr. Illingworth as that of his wife, Clara Ming worth, aged 28 years. Further search,until after dark,disclosed FIO further deaths. The bodies were removed to a tem- porary mortuary behind the King's Arms Hotel. On the young man's clothes being searched,among other articles were found a number of visiting cards bearing the name " H. Priestley, Rose Mount, Wyke." .uorecambe is peculiarly unfortunate in the matter of accidents, inasmuch as it is only just a year ago (on S -ptember 3rd, 1894) that the terrible boating accident occurred leading to the terrible loss of 25 lives. That vent quite spoiled the end of the season so far as More. came was concerned, and cast quite a groom over the community. Similarly the event of alouday is expected to have a deterrent effect on visitors.

The list of killed and injured is as follows : KILLED.

Jane Ann Ralph, aged 63, 37, Cavendish-place, Carlisle,

, widow. Herbert Priestley, aged about 25, Rose Cottage, Wyke, near Bradford. Claraillingworth, .aged 26, married, School House,

Mickletown, Methley, near Leeds. INJURED. Elizrbeth Saunders, widow, 24 Victoria-st., Morecambe fractured wrist, scalp wound and lacerated finger. Elizabeth Othrick, 30, Spring Gove-st., Towaite Gate, Gate, Hunslet—compound fracture of right leg. Elizabeth Ayre, 14, July-st., Manchester —injury to arm, Annie Ayre, aged 13, sister to above—injury to face. Miss Hilton, Irwell-place, Lower Brougnton—brought . .

out of sea apparently dead, but now convalescent. Mrs. Hunt, Laura-st., Lower Broughton—shaken,suffer- ing from shock. Several others, chiefly ladies, are also under medical treatment suffering from shock.

CURIOUS STORY OF DUBLIN CASTLE.

THE TORY BOYCOTT OF LORD CREWE.

The " Westminster Gazette" of Tuesday evening con- tained the following : A story has reached us which will give some idea of the energy and determination with which the Tory boy- cotting of the Earl of Crewe (Lord Houghton) when Viceroy of Ireland in the late Administration was organised, and the sanctions with which the system was enforced and its infringement punished. Viscount Charlemont, who is better known as Colonel James Caulfield, was in 1866, during the Tory Viceroy- alty of the late Duke of Abercorn, appointed to the post of Comptroller to the Household of the Lord-Lieutenant of Ireland. The office is held at the please of the Vice- roy, and on the retircment of a Lord-Lieutenant the Comptrollership becomes ipso facto vacant. Colonel Caulfield, however, notwithstanding his Toryism, which was of the most uncompromising character, discharged the duties of his office with such efficiency that he has been since his first appointment,nearly thirty years ago, retained in his position by successive Vicsroys. About four years ago Colonel Caulfield succeeded on the death of a kinsman to the ancient Irish peerage of Charlemont,created in the reign of James I. A vacancy in the Irish representative peerage occurred shortly after wards, and the suggestion that Lord Charlemont should be put for ward for election to a seat in the House of Lords as an Irish representative peer was well received, and no doubt of his selection entertained. Meanwhile, however, the defeat of the Tory Government at the general election of 1892 led to a change in the Lord- Lieutenancy, and the substitution of a Home Rule for a Tory Viceroy. Lord Charlemcnt was, as a matter of course, confirmed by Lord Crewe, the incoming Vice- roy, in his old position. He was, however, given to un- derstand that his declining to accept re-appointment by the Home Rule Viceroy would be highly popular in Unionist circles, and his name on his re-appointment as Comptroller was not put forward for election as an Irish representative peer. On the resignation of the late Government, Lord Charlemont happened to mee- Lord Cadogan, whose appointment to the Lurdt Lieutency of Ireland had then been settled in London. There was a long and friendly conversa- tion between them in reference to the manage- ment of the Viceregal establishment, with which Lord Charlemont's long experience had m..de him su familiar, and Lord Cadogan made then no suggestion that a change in the Controllership was contemplated. Lord Charlemont accompanied Lord Crewe on his de- parture in state from Dublin Castle to England, and intended to accompany Lord Cadogan on his coming over to Ireland to be sworn into the Lord Lieutenancy. On board ship, however, when crossing the Irish Chan- nel with the outgoing Lord-Lieutenant,Lord Cnarlernont received his first intimation that he was not to be re- tained in the office of Controller, which was conveyed to him ins letter written by Lord Cadogan to Lord Crewe, asking the latter nobleman to intimate to Lord Charlemont that new arrangements had been made with reference to the Controllership, and that perhaps Lord Charlemont would rather send in his resignation than be superseded—an error on Lord Cadogan's part, as the Controller ceases to hold office on the retirement from the Viceroyalty of the Lord Lieut enant by whom he was appointed. These matters have only gradually leaked out in conversation in Dublin circles. Lord Charlemont's own explanation of his own dismissal is noteworthy. " I was," he observed to a friend, " too much of a Home Ruler for them." The new Controller of the Household, Lord Longford, is an Irish peer. His peerage, however, does not date,like Lord Charlemont's, from 1820, but from 1800, the year of the Union. It was bestowed tor" Union services "in the Irish House of Commons.
