THE CONDITION OF Tag PARISEI OF STOCKTON HEA.TII. Sir,-1 should think there is not in Great Britain a 'parish in such a miserable condition as the Parish of Stockton Heath near Warrington. Here we have a Vicar who, poor rnan,hasbeen in such a delicate state of health.that he has not been able to do anything for years past, conscqufmtly he ti,s had to appoint a curate-in- charge to do his duty. The Vicar I believe has very little private means, and he cannot afford ti retire be- cause if he did he woul 1 lose his stipend, some £l3O a year. The miserable offertory of about £3a week on an average,has to pay the ciirat e-in-charge. The last curate only stayed two years,and was very sorry he ever cl me near the place. Mr. Vernon has worked hard for two years to try and improve the condition of the people, but has been constantic m t with this difficnity,that he had no authority in the parish, the sole authority being the Vicar, who from ill-health has not been able to do any- thing tor his pi rishiotters. Mr. Vernon is a conscientious hard-working clergy man, and is an eloquent and splendid preacher, but he feels like a man with his bands tied behind his back,as he has had no power and n authority, and at last he has been compelled from his own self respect to retire, and last Sunday he preached an eloquent, beautiful,and touching sermon, bidding farewell to the congregation, and to the

parish. I am told that all Bishops and Archbishops put together are not able to remedy this scate of affairs— that the law of the church prevents them ! Then I say the sooner the law is altered the better, and the sooner the church is separated from the State, the better it will be for religion and for the influence of the Church in England. Why tho very :tones in the street if they had voices would proclaim against such a shameful state of affairs. As someone said the other day, if such a condi- tion arose in a Nonconformist Church, the mischief would be cured in a week. The only manufactory of any size in the Parish is a large Brewery, and that as everybody knowe dues not exert a powerful religious influene.e ; on the contrary its tendency is to degrade and demoralise the working men and women. If anyone wishes to know the condition of Stockton Heath, let him ask the last Curate. Ido not believe that any core-cientious clergyman with any ability and any energy will stay more than 2 years in the Place. How long is this state of affairs to go on P—Yours, &C. COILNION SENSE. September sth, 1895.

RESIGNATION OF lIIE CHIEF CONSTABLE. Sir,—Tha resignation of Mr. Samuel Hunt, as chief Constable of the borough of Warrington, is an event in its history which may well excite considerable interest among its older and more thoughtful inhabitants. Statis- tically and socially the differences between the Warring- ton of his earlier and later experiences are great, and he has personally had much to do in the moulding and pro- duction of the presen- satisfactory condition of things. When he came a, the close of 1863 the population was only about 26,000, and the police force 16, and its mem- bers were on sect) easy terms with the publicans of the town that many of them could go in at one door and out at anther of most of their houses, levy toll on the passage and see nothing but what was strictly correct or required reporting while on the kairney. Mr. Hunt came as superintendent of the police force, to revolution- iSe the natural product of this state of things. And what with only a partially converted Watch Committee, with a somnolent magistracy, an obstructive chief con- stable, a recalcitrant body of officials, and a formidable and ill-regulated criminal element among the population, he found no bed of roses to receive him; and he had to fight his way into the respect, it not into the affections of his varir d antagonists. And the marvel was that the badgered superintendeot did not throw up his appoint- ment, and have his enemies masters of the field. But there were law-loving men in the town who encouraged ' him to p-rsevere ; and they had good materials to work on, and most satiafactory have been the results. Mr. Hunt was born a pAceman ; and he was uncon- cionsly qualifying himself for his harassing duties in Warrington by a careful study of the following rules for conduct, while acting with the Derby police force : Persevere again4t discouragement, keep your t-rnp.r, employ leisure in study, and always have some work on hand, be punctual and methodical in business, and never procrastinate; never be in a hurry, preserve self-posses- sion, and do not be talked out of a conviction. Rise early and he an economist of time, maintain dignity without the appearance of pride. Manners is something with everybody and everything with some. Be guarded in discourse,attentive and slow to speart,never acquiesce in immoral or pernicious opinions, be not forward to assign reasons to three who have no right to ask for them,think nothing in conduct unimportant or indiffer- ent,rather set than follow examples, practice strict tem- perance,and in all your transations remember the final account. May God add His blessing on me ! is the devout close of this admirably sin.gestive code of rules. And their observance has brought Mr. Hunt peace at the last. His charge has grown to a population of about 57,000 and to a police forceof b 4, and all are in admi- rable order. Without being demonstrative., the head of the police force has been practically übiquitous ; and when for cogent reasons the trae for retire- ment arrives the Mayor and Watch Committee " accept with regrel% the resignation of Mr. Runt, and in doing so desire to place on record thtir high ap- preciation of the efficient and satisfactory manner it; which he has during a period of 29 years discharged the duties of the wry important office of chief conqa hie of the borough that a testimonial to the value of £5O he ()resented to Mr. HUtlt QU his retirement from the office of Chief flonstaqe •thi that for the Purpose of the Police Acts, 1839 to 1893, the seevicea of Mr. Hunt in the force be eerVfi-ii to be approved service." Mr. Hunt, at the close of his resignation, asks "for the grant of the retiring pension to which he is legally entitled," and this request shows the mettle of the man we are lAng. He wants his own and no more. Long lifeand happiness te him ; and, in the interests of this town, an equally good man as his successor.—Yours truly, GEORGE POWLSON. Ex-Editor "Warrington Advertiser." THE NEW SE TIONAL MEMBER FOR WARRINGTON AND HIS PROMISES. Sir,—The great Brewer, who for so many years, repre- sented the " ehuroh " and I;quor trade of Warrington in the House of Commons, was gathered to his fathers some time ago, and notwi'hstanding all the blessings which he conferred upon the local clergy and their flocks his memory has been allowed to perish with his clay. The peculiar feature of the late member's Parliamentary career was profound silence: he was never known to open his month in debate upon any subject. Whether his successor is a I nilder of synagogues I know not, but I have evidence befnre me to show that he is not going to be a "dummy member "—not he. His voice will be heard with Apple use ;n the council of the nation, and the consequences will be -ah, who can tell ! He has made a beginning already, for the newspapers tell us that a few years ago he opened his mouth and sn ike to the Commons with such thrilling Pi^quence that the great Chamberlain was almost moved to tears. And, gentle reader, what do You think was the burden of his speech ? Yon will say at once,sozne topic of inter- flit " the gond old town," hi a birthplace, of which he is so prnud ! Not en. Like Gallica he cared for none of these things. The soul of Robert rose high above local matters and snared away over the blue waters of the Meditenanean to find a congenial subject in the island of icy rug. and here, my gentle reader, is a Press report of his great effsrt ! Mr. Piernoint drew attention to the , mannerin which the revenue of the island of Cyprus was ' seized trey," year for the purpose of paying the guarantees entered into by England and Prance in connection with , the island. We have taken from Cyprus nearly half of the whole revenue, and allowed the Cypriotes to spend £lO,OOO a year on their public works." Truly the blush of shame must have coloured the speaker's cheeks when he uttered such a damning commentary on British justice and fair play to a small and defenceless people. But surely the member's charity shorikl have com- menced at home and not so far away as Cyprus. There is flinty, of injugtier., over taxation end other wrongs afflicting his own constituents to afford abundant scope for the developments of his sneakily! plwers ! Saviour of Warrington Society he silent ! The blessings of three acres of lard and a cow were aa nothing com- pared to what was offered to the electors. By Sornfq un• explained And unexplainable process the kindly fruits of the earth were to spring up in such abundance that the farmers and their lahnurers would be so mutually bene- fitted that tt gy wink] sing together for joy ; kcal trade Would be sn increased by the mere prospect of docks to toe built hal a century her ce that the Mersey and the

In all seriousness, let me conclude by remarking that Mr. Pierpoint is only a very ordinary illustration of the political partisan whose tread scatters dust in the eyes of the ignorant and unwary, and ' whose mouth is often found uttering large and attractive promises which there is neither the will nor the power to perform. This double dealing was the rock upon which the great Liberal party perishcA. That party was grand at crying out in the market places, " Peace, Retrenchment, and Reform," and nibbling at some minor abuses, w bile it utterly neglected to touch the huge iniquities connected with the land, " the Church" and the liquor traffic. And Mr. Pierpoint and his party will assuredly meet the same doom if they adopt the same line of legislation. A power is being gradually developed in the country that will reccgnise no party shibboleths, and no party leaders like my Lords Salisbury, Devonshire, and Chamberlain, who neither toil nor spin. The aim of that parts- will be to beat down monopolies of all kinds, both in Church and &ate, and to see that the fruits of industry shall be the re- ward of the industrious, and not of the idle and worth- less.—Yours truly, lONA.

THE OPENING OF PARR HALL.

DESCRIPTION OF THE NEW ORGAN. In view of the opening of Parr Hall, on Septem- ber 26th, when there will be two performances by the Warrington Musical Society, a few details of the past history of the enlarged and up-to-date instrument which has been placed in the Hall by the Society will be of interest. In the winter of 1834-5 a movement was set afoot for the purpose of raising subscriptions towards the purchase of an organ to be vested in trustees chtmen to represent the War- rington Musical Society so long as it existed. The proposal met with a ready response, and the sub- scription list was headed by Mr. J. Wilson-Patten (afterwards Lord Winmarleigh), and the Hon. Horace Powys who each gave ten guineas. In the list of subscribers were many well-known names. Messrs. Joseph Stubs and William Hall each con- tributed £5; Mr. John Guest and Mr. IL R. Pick- mere, £4; Messrs. W. Hadfield, George Furnival, jun.. John Edelsten and Sons, T. W. Hardy, Thomas lierfoot (Greenall and Buckton), Edmund Malley, William Heath. William Stubs, Thomas Hall,Thomat. Vere Bayne: Caldwell, Parker, and Company; T. Hurst, Samuel Hassalls, James Haddock,and Broad- bent, each £2. A large number contributed El, among them being Messrs. J. R. Pickmere, James Marson, Beamont, end J. Pickmere. The treasurer was Mr. John Rylands, jun., who is still connected with the Society. The trust deed for the organ, which was drawn up by Messrs. Greenall and Buckton, is dated April 30th, 1835. The whole of the cost ot the organ was not raised at the time, and a lean of .£6O was required to complete the pur- chase. In 1840, further subscriptions were soli- cited, and the debt was then paid off. The organ was kept in the meeting places of the Magical Society until 1865, when the Public Hall in Rylands-et., was erected. After the opening of that Hall, the organ was placed there, where it re- mained until 1892, when the Hall was sold to the present theatrical company. The organ was then taken to pieces and stored away until recently, when it has been erected in the new Parr Hall. For this purpose, it has been extensively repaired, re- newed and enlarged by Mes:rs. Gray and Davison, organ builders, 30, Upper Stanhope-et., Liverpool, at a cost of ahou 4350. In the trust deed, there were originally 38 trustees. all of whom are now de- ceased. On January 16, 1892, Lord Winmarleigh, who was the last of the original trustees,appointed a fresh batch of trustees. The Musical Society have been able since their decision to erect this new organ, to set an foot a subscription list. They have realised close upon .£2OO up to the present time. They hope within the next month or two to almost if not entirely cover the whole of the .£350 expended on the organ.

SPEXIFICATiON OF THE ORGAN. The organ has been greatly improved and extended since its erection in the Public Flail, and the appended specification will show the extent of its resources. The inerument may he considered new, exceptint; portions of the old sound boards and pipes, which have been in- corporated in the present instrument. Much of the old work was found to be snund and good thus testifying the excellent quality of the work done by Gray and Davison in 1872. The present trint is designed and made to meet modern requirements. The various tiers of pipes are supplied with direct wind by means of tubu- lar pneumatics, which greatly increases the volume of tone and dispenses with many complications. The manuals and couplers are built up-n-1 an improved prin- ciple, each separee pip o having sufaci,mt speaking space and ample room is provided for the combined full organ. The Musical Saciety deserve all credit for their enterprise in thus providinci an efficient organ in time for the open- 13g of the new Hall.

Bn. from Mr. George Crosfield, being the proceeds of per- formance given in the Bank Quay Schoolroom on Satur- day evening in aid of the Institute Funds. THE NEW CURATE.—A successor to till Rev. H. EARLESTOWN EBENEZER LODGE.

THE SONS OF TEMPEHANES.

On Wednesdav evening the Cadet of Temperance of the Samuel Morley section met in the Co-opera- tive Hall, Bro. A. Smith in the chair. After open- ing a brother gave a short address. At 8 o'clock the adult division met in the same room, Bro. John Pickett presiding. After the opening the question of the anniversary was discussed and October 2nd was decided for tea and entertainment. A sub- committee was appointed to draw up a programme for the coming quarter and business relating to the order was discussed. A pleasant meeting was closed at 9.30. DAHLIA SHOW AT THE VULCAN INN.

On Saturday a line show ot dahlias was held at ' the Vulcan, which had been carefully selected from various growers in Lancashire and I..lheshire, by 40 competitors anxious to secure the prizes offered for the best specimens. Each exhibitor had on view three distinct varieties, the majority being very fine indeed. Messrs. R. Andrew and L. Bees- ton were the judges, and awarded the prizes as fol- lows :—l, J. Lewis. VOLUNTRER SHOOTING MATCH.

Having received a challenge from the isle of Man detachment, the Newton detachment of the 6th Liverpool (King's) Battalion selected a team, con- sisting of Sergeant Bateman, Lance-Corporal Rose, Lance-Corporal Rimmer, Sergeant-Instructor Bird, and Privates Gibson, Wallace, Pullen and Appleton, to represent them in the struggle. The team journeyed to Douglas on Saturday, and met with a hearty reception from their brotherly rivals. Ou Sunday they R ent for a dtive, and the match was shot off on Monday morning. Newton won by 25 points, the whole of the team putting on good scores. The four o'clock boat homeward bound was boarded, the trip having been thoroughly enjoyed. The return will be decided at Newton during the next fortnight.

END OF THE WIGAN MINERS' STRIKE. Work was resumed on tiredne,day at No. 2 pit of the Garswood Mall Colliery Company at Brynn,near Wigan, where about 400 miners and datallers have been on strike during the past three weeks. The firm wanted the men to work the coal on the end ' instead of on the face, and the miners declined without an alteration of payment,as they contended that the new system was more difficult. On Tues- day Mr. Sam Woods, Mr. Thomas Greenall, and other of the Lancashire miners' leaders met the , management of the colliery, and after a conference extending over five hours a compromise was arrived it which the men accepted. During the stoppage the miners received 10s. per week and Is. per head for the members of their families from the funds of the Lancashire Miners' Federation. GOoD TEMPLARY.

The weekly session of the hope of Earlestown Lodge ot Good Templets was held oa Monday even- ing presided over by Bro. Hatton, C.T. There was a good attendance. Francis, of St. Helens, ale.-; sergeant Roberts, of Warrington. Vctes of thanks were tendered to the Sisters, and the meeting ter- minated. WARGRAVB CHURCH SUNDAY SCHOO'S.

On Saturday at the Millstone Inn, an exhibition lit plants, flowers, fruit and vegetables was held in a large room connected with that place. The show which is to be an annual affair was promoted by Mr. Webster, the landlord, who offered a number of enticing prizes open to all competitors. The ex- hibits were excellently staged, and altogether num- bered 328. belonging to 31 exhibitors. Competitif n was very keen, and the quantity of products was of an all round excellence. Messrs. Watkins, of ilaydock, and Norman,of Golborne, acted as judges. A finely developed vegetable marrow (not for com- petition), attracted no little attention, and its solidity was vouched for by the fact that it scaled 271bs. The awards consisted of kettles, tea pots, and money prlz.s, the expense of which—with one exception—contributed by Councillor Simpson,was borne by Mr. Webster. SOCIETY.

A general meeting of the members of this society was held in the Town Hall, Newton, on Friday evening. Dr. J. W. Watkins presided and about 50 members attended. The Chairman, in opening the proceedings, remarked that it had been propo,ed that such a society should he formed and with that object a committee ha I been got together to arrange matters for their first. meeting. From the number of persons who had signified their intention of be- coming members, he thought that society would prove a great success. (Hear, hear.) Over SO had joined. CO being vocalists and the remainder instru- mentalists. The Hon. T. W. Legh, M.P., had con- sented to become their president. Messrs. J. W. Glover and Ilindley Smith had undertsken the duties of joint-secretaries, and together with the lommittee had made the preliminary arrangements and convened that meeting. A code of rules would be submitted for approval. Mr. J. W. Glover then submitted the rules, which were taken seriatim and unanimously agreed to. The election of officers resulted as follows:—President, the Hon. T. W. Legh, M.P.; vice-president. Dr. .T. W. Watkins ; treasurer, Captain Royse, R.N.; secretaries, Messrs. Hindley Smith and J. W. Glover; committee, Mrs. Rayse, Mrs. E. T. ',:vans, Miss Watkins, Councillor Smith, Mr. J. Singleton, Mr. T. Wilkinson, Mr. P. Fairclough ; conductor, Mr. F. A Crossley of War- rington. It was resolved to commence rehearsing Gaul's composition "Joan of Arc," the first meeting to take place, in the St. Peter's Schoolroom on Thursday, October 3rd, at eight o'cloclt.

CYCLES,—GARLAND, Sole Agent, Bridge-st., Warring on. _ _ .

From a statement of receipts and payments made up to the 30. h June this year, in connection with this new church, there is still owing to the bank on overdraft, the sum of £231 ss. 4d., " to which must be added, say, another .130 for further expenses of painting, &c.. and in addition there will be the bank interest running until the overdraft be written off." It is further stated that " one of the require- ments of the Bishop prior to the consecration of new churches in the diocese of Liverpool is, that a repair fund shall be established equal in amount to five per cent. on the total cost of the church. On the consecration of this church, however, seeing that the building fund was not all subscribed, the committee were not able to meet this require- ment., but, by- special arrangement, they obtained the postponement of the requirement on the under- standing that an effort would be made as soon as possible to raise the amount required for such fund. 'in, repair fund in this case will be close upon .£300." The total amount required is, therefore, £541 54. 41. The total subscriptions received up to the 30th June amounted to £5,362 2s. 7d.

SEwisok MACH/NZ9, Hand zz Los. ou. Treadle £4 4s. with I handsome walnut cover.—J. and W. DurroN, 16 an 13, Sankey•st., Warrinirton.—Advt. THE BUTTERMILK BRIDGE FATALITY. On Sionday evening, Mr. Brighouse, county icoroner, held an inquest at the Bowling Green Inn, Bank Quay, on the body of Christopher Taylor, 12 sears of age, who lived with his parents at 4,Selbv- street, and who was drowned in the St. Helens and 'Sankey Canal near Buttermilk Bridge, on Friday night.—Thomas Preston Taylor, father of the , deceased, stated that the lad left his home about Ifive o'clock on Friday evening, and the next he saw of him was when he was brought home drowned, at about 6.30 the same evening.—Clyde Crooks stated that he with deceased and others went to play near the bridge, and the deceased in making an attempt to walk along the rail of the bridge fell in. He (witness) was then about ten yards away, and he ran to his friend, who was struggling in the water, and tried to pull him out with his i 3.0.1, 'but was unable to do so. He then sent his brother, James Crooks,to the deceased's home,to inform his parente of the occurrence.—James Crooks proved sec,lng the decea,ed fall into the canal,and on being questioned by the Coroner, said that he had often tried to walk on the bridge rail.—Thomas Knox stated that he was passing along toe bank at the time of the acci- dent, and he succeeded in getting the body from the water with a grappling iron, but life was then quite extinct The body was then removed home.—The jury, after a long consultation on the defi-cts of the bridge, returned a verdict of " Accidentally drowned."—..' be Coroner remarked that he would send the following presentment to the officials of I the London and North-Western Railway Company, to whom the bridge belongs :—" September 1895. Deceased drowned in canal whilst walking along the rail of the bridge. It is hoped that the rails on each side of the bridge be boarded up, and that the rails of the bridge be made V shaped, so that no person can walk along them."l h.rdsvoimso Mws.,oElnuitsesco,,Hers—nas £2. nI.BIW. RE-OPENING OF THE PADGITE INSTITUTE. The annual meeting of the members of the Church Institute was held in the Church House, Padgate, on Monday evening. The Vicar, the Rev. J. J. Longhorn, MA., presided, and there was a large attendance of members. Mr. S. H. Antrobus (the secretary), said that his report for the past , season was more satisfactory than any previous one ' he had had the pleasure to submit, owing to the growing of the institute in various ways. They ' commenced the session with a membership of 96, ! which hail grown considerably. The most interest- event during the last session was the opening of the Church House by the members on the 4th February. After having referred to several other events of the session, the Secretary stated that a most successful season was brought to a close by a smoking concsrt on the sth May. On the proposi- tiJn of Mr. T. 11. Granger. seconded by Mr. T. S. Steel, the report was adopted. The Treasurer's (Mr. J. P. Critchley) accounts, having been audited and found correct,, were passed. The total income amounted to .Cl 2 Os. Sl., and expenditure to £lO 10s. 2d. The Rev. J. J. Longhorn was re-elected pre- sident, and the Rev. W. R. flartwright and Messrs. J. W. Besivers, F`. M. Appleton, 13 Mather, 11. M. Mines, A. Bennett and W. Ilodgkinson vice-presi- dents, with the addition of the name of Mr. J. Gib- son. Mr. T. 11. Granger was elected secretary, and Mr. James P. Critchley treasurer. The committee ' was elected by hallot,and resulted in the following order :—Messrs. S. H. Antrobus, Thomas S. Steel, John Monks, J. R. Jones, William Weston,T. Smith, E. Howard, and Joseph Weston. Mr. Steel proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. S. H. Antro- bus for his services as secretary of the institute during the past five years. The Rev. J. J. Longhorn seconded, and Mr. J. P. Critchley supported the proposition, which was carried unanimously. Mr. Antrobus said what he had done for the institute he had done willingly, and it had been a pleasure to him. A vote of thanks to Mr. Critchley for his services as treasurer during the past session and to the Vicar for presiding brought the meeting to a close. THE LEAGUE OF THE CROSS.

A most suecessful meeting of this Society, was held in the League Hall, Bswsey-street, on Sunday evening, Mr. John Mullins, president, in the chair. In the course of an earnest and instructive address, Mr. Mullins expressed his gratitude to the gentle- man who so kindly sent the very eloquent eulogium of him and his work to the " Examiner" last week. He said he had been engaged in the work of tbe League of the Cross for over twenty-six years; and he was desirous that when he was gone someone would be able to tako his place in that work, and to that end he wished to endeavour to create speakers by founding a debating class and to train young men in the art of speaking add likewise in the art of writing papers oil questions of the day. He would endeavour in future to make these meet- ings as pleasant, as bright, and as instructive as possible. The meeting was next addressed by Mr. J. C. Dalton, who dealt with the persecution of the early Uhristians under the Emperor Maximian. He described in vivid colours the scenes enacted in the Roman arena, and in explaining how the martyrs received the Holy Viaticum, on the night previous to their martyrdom, he gave a beautiful sketch of 11 the heroic death of the holy acolyte Tarcisius, the I here of the Blessed Sacrament, who was kicked and beaten to death by a Roman mob, because he refused to give up the Holy Eucharist to be profaned by the Pagans. Tarcisius had volunteered to convey the Holy Viaticum to the imprisoned Christians, a task which almost al- ways meant death to him who undertook it; and he gave up his young life,like the child Agnes,like the blind girl Csecillia,the valiant soldier Sabastian,and the holy Roman youth Pancras, for Christ in oppo- sition to Casiar ! In conclusion, Mr. Dalton exhorted his hearers to profit by the beautiful examples set them by their sisters and brothers in the faith, the holy martyrs and confessors. They were not asked to die for,their faith, but they could work and live for it, by bringing new members into the League of the Cross, and thereby making them practical Catholics. Several men took the abstinence pledge at the conclusion of the pleating.

PENKETH.

WESLEYAN CHAPEL.-.--051 Sunday last the annual har- vest thanksgiving services were held in the above chapel. There were two services, morning and evening. The chapelmaa very artistically decorated with flowers,fruit, and corn, contributed by the members of the congrega- tion. Wigfield:officiated at the organ, Suitable hymns were rendered by the choir.

ALLEGED THEFT OF FOWLS AT SUTTON.

COMMITTAL OF THE PRISONER.

On Wednesday morning before Mr. E. unison, at Run- corn Police Court, Alexandra Jackson, of Volunteer-st., Newtown,Frodsham,was brought up on a charge of fowl stealing, on September Bth. at Sutton. the fowls being the property of Hannah Bate. Mr. Brassey, Chester, appeared for the prisoner. P.C. Price, of Frodsham,stated that about 1.15 on the morning a September 9th, he met the prisoner while on duty on the Chester Main-road, near Frodsham Bridge, eoming from the direction of Sutton. He had a fowl under his left arm. Witness stopped him and on search- ing him found he had two more fowls inside the breast of his shirt. He asked him where be got them frorn,and he replied " They are my motheis." Witness disbeliev- ing him said " You will have to come with me." He then took prisoner to Frodsham Pelle() Station, and de- tained him. Later on the accused said, "I might as well tell you the truth ; I got them from Mr. Lowe's, at Sutton Hall." The fowls on him were quire warm, and seemed to have been re- cently killed.—Edwin Bate, son of Mrs. Hannah Bate Sutton Weaver,said he lived with her and managed the farm. On Sunday night about ten o'clock he heard the hens matting a noise,and two of the workmen said there must be someone among them. He went to the door, but it being very dark, they could see no one. Next morning,fromiwnat his sister said he examined the fowls, and three were misting. In the afternoon Sergeant Ben- field called at the farm, and witness went to Frodsharn Police Station where he saw three dead fowls, which he identified as those belonging to his mother. He had seen them alive on the farm on Sunday. They were wor h about 84.—8 y Mr. Brassey : They bad more than 100 fowls on the farm. He knew the hens because of their size. They were taken from the garden and not from the hen house.

P.S. Benfield, stationed at Sutton, said that when prisoner was charged with stealing he replied " I am not guilty of stealing from Hannah Bates, but am guilty of stealing from William Lowe's. I will tell the truth if I am to be hung."—The magistrate intimated that the prisoner would be committed to the Sessions for

A second case, that of stealing three hens, belonging to William Lowe ot Sutton Hall, on September Bth, was introduced. Mr. Lowe stated that on Monday he missed abote five fowls of different sorts and colours. Sergeant Benfield saw him on Monday morning and on the fol- lowing day he proceeded to Frodsham Police Station, where he saw three fowls, but could not identify them. —lnspector Hicks said that was as far as he could go in the second case, and under the circumstances he would not proceed further with it. Mr. Brassey then applied for his client to be admitted to bail in a reasonable amount.—lnspector Hicks bad no objection if the bail was substantial, as prisoner had a record against him. Unfortunately for him be bad been tried at the Quarter Sessions and convicted for sheep stealing. Under the circumstances he might not take it kindly to go to the Quarter Sessions for fowl stealing, if there was a chance to slip away.—The Bench did not consider the case one in which they could grant bail.

SERIOUS CHARGE AGAINST A FORMER RUNCORN TRADESXCAN.

SEQUEL TO BETTING TRANSACTIONS. On T,resday evening at Runcorn Police Court, before Mr. F. J. Norman and E. Imison,Jarnes Tinsley, formerly carryin . on business as a jeweller and watchmaker in Bridge..s. reef, Runcorn, was charged on remand that, whilst the bailee of a gold watch,the property of Edwin Gregory, arncer,Bridge-street, he fraudulently converted tn same to kis own use and stole the same on Octobi-r 12th.

Inspector Hicks explained that he proposed to take three charges, and he expected that if prima facie cases were made out, the bench would commit prisoner upon them. He asked for three7cases to be taken because one differed somewhat from the others. In two cases the pawn tickets as well as the watches left in the hands of Tinsley for reoairs after being pledged, were returned to the owners and consequently had been recovered.ln the third instance the owner had neither recovered his watch nor the pawn ticket—it was gone totally, and therefore no defence could be brought in that case that he pawned it with intent to redeem it. Ed win Gregory,grocer and provision dealer, 22 Bridge. street, stated that in October last year the prisoner was in business as a jeweller and watchmaker in Bridge- street. In September he gave the gold watch producest to Tinster, for a few necessary repairs. Ile repeatedly applied for the watch in the course of several weeks but failed to get it. Then he heard that prisoner had left the town and this was followed by a sale of the goods in the establishment. He received a letter about this time through post purporting to come from Tinsley, en- closing the pawnticket produced, which had reference to a gold lever watch, pledged with Frecerick James Walter, of London-road, Liverpool, in the name of John Farnworth-street. He placed the matter in the hands of Mr. Lakg, solicitor, with the result that, he again obtained possession of the watch. The prisoner bad no authority to pledge it. Frederick James Walter. pawnbroker, 194, London. road, Liverpool, stated that the watch in question was pledged with him on October 12th, 1894, by a man who gave his name as John Williams, of Farnworth-street, Liverpool. He believed the prisoner to be the same man, for on previous occasions he had pledged articles with him. The duplicate ticket produced bore the name of the pledger. In consequence of some legal correspon- dence he gave up the watch on April 6th last.—By the prisoner : He believed his brother had been to see him with reference to paying for the watch. P.C. Gregory deposed-to apprehending the prisoner a', 8 p.m., en September 6th, at 7, Nursery-st., Fairfield, Liverpool. He read over the warrant to him, charging him with the offence when he replied "That is true, I am sorry for it, it is all through company." On the way to Runcnrn, whilst in the train, they had some conver- sation together, and he told him it was all through bet- ting,. He had a telegram to go nap on a horse. He had only £3 in the shop,and toak a watch and pledged it for another £3, and went and backed the horse which lost. The man called for the watch in pledge and he told him it was not quite ready. He also said that if the bailiffs had not been put in the house he would have paid everything. Another charge was preferred against the prisoner, thst on October 19th, he converted a gold watch value £lB to his own use, the watch belonging to W. Moores, Carrier. William Moores identified the gold watch produced as his. In September he gave it to the prisoner for repairs, and it being there some weeks he applied for it several times. On one occasion Tinsley brought the watch to him, but said he would like to keep it for a few days to see how it went. He afterwards heard of prisoner leav- ing the t9wo and the subsequent sale of his goods. On November 26th he received the letter produced and the pawn ticket enclosed from prisoner. Witness handed the pawn ticket to his solicitor who recovered the watch. Prisoner hal no authority to pledge it. Robert Hearts., assistant to Mr. Handley Bradley, pawnbroker, 14 and 16, Brook-street, Chester, stated that the watch produced was pledged with him by the prisoner, on October 19th last, for £6 in the name of William Young, 112, Greenway-road, Runcorn. P.C. Gregory said he charged the prisoner with the offence that morning and he replied " Yes, that's it." During the conversation in the train when coming from Liverpool he said, if he had not been put in the County Court he could have got the watches out, paid for every- thiing, and continued his business having got together a n ce connection.—ln reply to prisoner witness said that he (prisoner) did say that he had come from America to to have the case settled as he could not rest. - _

~„ After a ronaul'ation between the Beech and Deputy Clerk to the Magistrates, and Inspector Hicks, the latter said he would for the present withdraw the third charge he had intended to prefer against accused.—The Chair- man then stated they had decided to commit prisoner for trial at t Le next Cheshire Quarter Sessions to be held on October 14th. They were sorry to see a respectable tradesman in such a position, but he would receive fair trial at the hands of his own countrymen. The brother of the accused applied for him to be liber- ated on bail, his father and father-in-law being ready to act as sureties. Inspector Hicks said that if the request was granted the sum should be of a substantial charac- ter. The bench decided to accept hail, prisoner in £lOO in each case, and two sureties of £5O in each case.

FINANCIAL NEWS.

-- In a recent issue we imparted to our readers the fact that we had heard good news about North Sheba. The shares were then about 53., and although the price has risen to about 75., they are still strongly recommended for a considerable improvement. The recent rich find on the Company's property is said to have assayed as high as 9rza. to the ton, and the matter was considered of such importance that the quartz was placed on view at the " Goldfields News" office at Barberton, and at- tracted many interested visitors.—" Mining Herald," September 7th, 1895.

Amongst best speculative investments in the South- African market at the present moment are the shares of the Randt Reefs Company. This Company owns 115 claims about four miles from Johannesburg and five valu able gold reefs run through the entire property. It is also more than probable that the main reef traverses the property at a workable depth. The manager reports most favourably upon the prospects saying that with the enormous quantity of ore on the claims, which can be easily and cheaply grassed, the company should be very quickly placed amongst the dividend payers. The Ishares now stand at about 16s. each. The possibilities are therefore great and it is not so long ago that East Rands were about the same price, whilst te day they are 81. Randt Reefs should be bought now for a similar advance.

laSr F. West Ridgeway, Lieutenant Governor of the Isle of Man, has been appointed Governor of Ceylon, as sue- cescor to Sir A. E. Havelock, appointed Governor of Madras.
